The same exact tarball installs correctly via `npm install ask-llm-mcp` to a project directory — verified by running doctor against a fresh `/tmp/ask-llm-repro` directory install on the same Node 26.0.0 binary, which works perfectly. The bug is **specifically in npm 11's global install + `bundledDependencies` interaction**. The original bundling mechanism (ADR-052) was introduced to ship `@ask-llm/shared` (a `private: true` workspace package) inside each published MCP server's tarball, working around npm 9's inability to parse the `workspace:` protocol in published manifests. That fix has been load-bearing for the v1.5.7 → v1.6.5 release line. But the `bundledDependencies` mechanism is what triggers the npm 11 global-install bug; without it, the bug class disappears entirely. **The architectural question this ADR resolves: do we keep `@ask-llm/shared` private and try to work around the npm 11 bundling bug (by bundling all transitive deps, ~5MB tarballs), or do we make `@ask-llm/shared` a normal public npm package and eliminate the bundling mechanism that triggered the bug?**
- **Decision:** Make `@ask-llm/shared` a public npm package and remove the bundling mechanism entirely. Concrete changes: (a) `packages/shared/package.json` — remove `"private": true`, add `"publishConfig": { "access": "public" }`, add `"mcpName"`, `"description"`, `"keywords"`, `"author"`, `"license"`, `"repository"`, `"bugs"`, `"homepage"`, `"engines"`, `"files"` metadata matching the pattern from `packages/gemini-mcp/package.json`. Add `LICENSE` file alongside the existing `README.md`. (b) All four MCP packages (`gemini-mcp`, `codex-mcp`, `ollama-mcp`, `llm-mcp`) — delete the `bundledDependencies` array and the `prepack` + `postpack` scripts that invoked `scripts/prepack-bundle.mjs` / `scripts/postpack-restore.mjs`. (c) `scripts/prepack-bundle.mjs` + `scripts/postpack-restore.mjs` — delete both. Yarn 4's built-in publish behavior already handles the `workspace:* → fixed-version` rewrite that ADR-052's script was doing manually (verified: `yarn workspace ask-llm-mcp pack` produces a tarball with `"@ask-llm/shared": "0.3.1"` rewritten from `workspace:*` automatically, and to the *exact* current workspace version, which is more precise than our script's `workspace:* → *` wildcard rewrite). (d) `docs/CONTRIBUTING.md` — update the "bundled-deps cascade" paragraph to describe the new flow (internal-dependents cascade still works via changesets' `updateInternalDependents: "always"`). (e) `.github/workflows/release.yml` — update the comment describing the publish lifecycle. (f) One changeset bumping all 5 packages (patch) with a body explaining the fix. **The publish ordering is correct**: changesets/action publishes in topological order, so `@ask-llm/shared` ships first, then the four MCPs that depend on it via `workspace:*` (rewritten to `0.3.2` or whatever version shared lands at). Once shared is on the npm registry, `npm install` / `npm install -g` / `npx -y ask-llm-mcp` all behave identically — npm fetches each package from the registry and resolves the dep tree the normal way, no bundling sleight-of-hand involved. **Tarball size impact**: `ask-llm-mcp` drops from 202 files (~80KB tarball) to 31 files (~17KB tarball); the four per-provider MCPs similarly shrink because they no longer inline `@ask-llm/shared/dist`. **Out of scope explicitly**: (a) Filing the npm bug upstream — worth doing as a follow-up, but the fix here doesn't depend on npm fixing the bundling issue. (b) Bundling all transitive deps as an alternative — considered and rejected: ~5MB tarballs, maintenance burden of keeping the bundled list in sync with transitive resolution, and the bundling pattern would still be vulnerable to future npm install-strategy changes. The "publish shared" path eliminates the bug class permanently. (c) Documenting a manual install workaround — considered and rejected: leaves the bug in the wild for global/npx users until they read the docs, and `npx` is the recommended setup path in `claude_desktop_config.json` examples. (d) Re-using ADR-052's mechanism for some hybrid where shared remains private but its deps are bundled — adds complexity without addressing the npm bug.
- **Consequences:** **What this gains:** (a) **#115 is fixed at the architecture level** — the entire bug class (npm install-strategy interaction with bundling) is eliminated, not just the specific zod symptom. (b) **Smaller, simpler tarballs** — orchestrator drops 6x; the publish flow becomes a straightforward "yarn build → changeset publish" with no custom scripts in the middle. (c) **Faster `npx -y ask-llm-mcp`** — npm caches `@ask-llm/shared` separately from `ask-llm-mcp`, so transitive cache hits compound across all four MCP packages that share the dep. Previously each MCP shipped its own copy bundled. (d) **One less thing that can break on future npm version changes** — `bundledDependencies` is a relatively obscure feature with multiple historical npm bugs (this issue is the second one we've hit; the first was the npm 9 workspace protocol issue that motivated ADR-052). Removing it removes our exposure to that class of bug. **What this costs:** (a) **One more public npm package to maintain.** `@ask-llm/shared` is already versioned by changesets (`privatePackages: { version: true }`), so the only mechanical change is that it now also publishes. The CHANGELOG and version-bump flow is unchanged. (b) **First public version of shared has no prior published reference point** — on the first publish, npm's deduplication for users upgrading to this release will see shared as a brand-new dep. No real consequence other than a slightly larger first install. (c) **A `workspace:*` rewrite is now silently performed by yarn 4** rather than by our visible custom script. Worth documenting (this ADR does so) so future contributors don't get confused by the lack of the bundling lifecycle. (d) **ADR-052's `prepack-bundle.mjs` + `postpack-restore.mjs` scripts and their rationale are now historical.** ADR-052 remains as the historical record of the original `workspace:*` problem and its first solution; ADR-106 supersedes the *mechanism* but preserves the *motivation* (npm 9 manifest parsing). The DECISIONS.md entry for ADR-052 is not modified — superseded ADRs stay in place as the project's institutional memory. **What does NOT change:** (a) Public APIs of any package — `ask-llm-mcp`, `ask-gemini-mcp`, `ask-codex-mcp`, `ask-ollama-mcp` expose the same MCP tools with the same signatures. (b) The Claude Code plugin (`@ask-llm/plugin`) — unaffected since it's distributed via marketplace, not npm, and doesn't use `bundledDependencies`. (c) The set of supported install paths — `npm install -g`, `npx -y`, `npm install` (local), `claude mcp add` all continue to work; the global/npx paths now actually work on Node 26. (d) Test count — no test changes; verification was that all 184 existing tests still pass after the package.json + script changes (verified: 13 test files / 156 tests in shared, plus the per-package test suites all green; yarn lint clean across all 6 workspaces). **Verification methodology**: (1) Reproduced #115 exactly on Node 26.0.0 + npm 11.12.1 (`npm install -g ask-llm-mcp@0.3.8` then `ask-llm-mcp doctor` → identical stack trace). (2) Inspected the broken install layout, identified 78 empty placeholder directories under `node_modules/`. (3) Confirmed local install (`npm install ask-llm-mcp` in a fresh project dir) works on the same Node 26 binary — establishing the bug is global-install-specific. (4) Manually populated the missing zod + sdk + transitive deps in the broken install, re-ran doctor, confirmed it works — proving the only fix needed is correct dep population. (5) Ran `yarn workspace ask-llm-mcp pack` after the architectural change, verified the resulting tarball has `workspace:*` rewritten to fixed versions and contains no bundled `node_modules/`. (6) Ran `yarn install` + `yarn build` + `yarn lint` + `yarn test` after the change, all green. The end-to-end verification (publish to npm, install -g on Node 26, run doctor) can only happen post-publish; this is the test the next release will run automatically. **Forward path:** (a) Land this PR, merge, let changesets/action publish 5 packages (shared first, then the four MCPs). (b) Verify post-publish that `npm install -g ask-llm-mcp@<new>` works on Node 26 — if the bug recurs (it shouldn't, but the bug pattern was non-obvious), the rollback is to add the bundling back and bundle ALL transitive deps. (c) File the underlying npm 11 global install + `bundledDependencies` bug upstream so other projects using bundling for monorepo workspace deps don't hit the same wall. (d) Update BUGS.md to mark #115 as resolved-pending-publish (then resolved-fixed once published). (e) Roadmap Tier A item closes; Tier B (parser defensiveness) becomes the next concrete PR target. **Forensic record**: the prepack-bundle.mjs script's git history will preserve the bundling mechanism for anyone who needs to understand how the project handled this between v1.5.7 and v0.3.8. The deleted scripts and the bundledDependencies entries are reachable via `git log --all -- scripts/prepack-bundle.mjs` once this PR lands.

## ADR-100: `codex-pair` Prompt A/B Benchmark Harness (Validates ADR-099 and Future Prompt Changes)
- **Date:** 2026-05-26
- **Status:** Accepted (draft methodology; harness scaffolded but not yet executed; complements ADR-077 / ADR-089 / ADR-099)
- **Context:** ADR-099 added a `## Baseline review principles` section to `prompts/review.txt`, adapting three Karpathy-derived rules (Simplicity, Surgical scope, Hidden assumptions). The integration is justified by the principles being well-articulated, but the ADR explicitly deferred the question of whether the change *empirically* improves codex's review output. The forward path in ADR-099 says: "If the baseline empirically improves recall, that's the validating evidence. If it doesn't, this ADR can be reversed in one PR." ADR-077's original four-task benchmark (todo CRUD, URL shortener, JSON Patch RFC 6902, shopping cart) lives on the `experiment/codex-pair-poc` branch and was structured to test *bug detection* (does codex-pair catch float-money precision / validation bypass / edge-case clamping?). That methodology doesn't transfer cleanly to ADR-099's question, which is about *prompt-rule detection* — does adding the baseline change what codex flags on a diff that contains a known baseline violation? Different question; needs a different harness. The harness also needs to be reusable: future prompt changes (severity-vs-urgency refactor, structured-output tweaks, baseline rule extensions) should have a low-friction way to demonstrate "this change earns its keep" before merging — without that, prompt evolution becomes "ship and see if anyone complains," which is exactly the lived-experience anti-pattern ADR-095 codified. **The methodology question this ADR resolves: what does "empirical validation of a prompt change" look like for codex-pair, and what's the minimum harness that delivers that signal cheaply enough to run on every prompt PR?**
- **Decision:** Ship a small A/B benchmark harness at `packages/claude-plugin/scripts/benchmark/` that takes two prompt templates (a "control" arm and a "treatment" arm), renders each against a fixed fixture set, invokes real `codex exec --json` for each rendered prompt, and emits a markdown report comparing recall against per-fixture ground truth. **Harness structure**: (a) `templates/` — vendored snapshots of the two prompts being compared (initially `pre-baseline.txt` matching main's prompt as of ADR-098, and `baseline.txt` matching ADR-099's prompt with the Karpathy block; future runs vendor new snapshots without modifying these). (b) `fixtures/` — directories named `NN-slug/` each containing `code.ts` (file content sent to codex), `context.md` (marker content sent as projectContext), and `probes.json` (ground-truth `should_flag` entries with expected severity and matching keywords, plus optional `should_not_flag` negative probes). (c) `lib/` — four focused helpers: `render-prompt.mjs` (mirrors `lib/prompt.mjs::buildReviewPrompt` substitution logic standalone so the benchmark doesn't depend on whichever prompt is currently in `prompts/review.txt`), `invoke-codex.mjs` (spawns `codex exec --json` with the same flags the real hook uses, parses JSONL output into the verdict shape, ~80 LOC), `score.mjs` (keyword-based probe matching — finding text must contain ≥2 of a probe's keywords to count as a match; crude but free), `report.mjs` (markdown generator). (d) `prompt-ab.mjs` — the driver: parses args (`--fixtures`, `--model`, `--out`, `--timeout-ms`), loads fixtures, runs both arms sequentially against each fixture, writes the markdown report. **Initial fixture set (four representative scenarios, each crafted to exercise one baseline rule):** (1) `01-overcomplication` — Claude's "task" was "add a `getFullName` function"; the diff implements it via a single-use NameFormatter interface + NameFormatterFactory class + unused `separator` / `locale` configurability + impossible-scenario runtime null checks against TypeScript types. (2) `02-drive-by-refactor` — task was a 3-line bug fix to `parsePagination`; the diff correctly fixes the bug but ALSO renames an unrelated function, reformats a constants block, and adds a history-commentary comment block — all surgical-scope violations. (3) `03-orphan-imports` — task was "remove the legacy `formatV1` function"; the diff removes it correctly but leaves `decodeBase64` and `logger` imports orphaned (only formatV1 used them). (4) `04-hidden-assumption` — task asked for "the EARLIEST unbooked slot from a list of `{startMinute, booked}` entries"; the diff returns the first by ARRAY ORDER, silently depending on the caller having pre-sorted by startMinute — a hidden invariant. **Decision rule for ADR-099 validation (the harness's first application):** ship ADR-099's change to `prompts/review.txt` permanently if recall delta (baseline arm minus pre-baseline arm) ≥ +10 percentage points AND extra-finding delta ≤ +1 finding per fixture on average. If recall regresses or noise jumps materially, execute the two-file rollback path ADR-099 documents. The harness writes this verdict line mechanically into the report so the merge decision doesn't require manual interpretation of the numbers. **Out of scope explicitly**: (a) Actually RUNNING the benchmark and recording its result in this ADR — the harness is scaffolded, not yet executed; running it costs ~$0.40 per full pass and produces data that should be reviewed and discussed before merging or reversing ADR-099. The actual run can happen any time after this PR lands. (b) An LLM-judge scoring mode (gemini evaluates whether each finding matches each probe in semantic — not keyword — sense) — tracked as a future enhancement once the keyword-match floor proves too crude for subtle rule wording. (c) CI integration (run the benchmark automatically on every PR touching `prompts/review.txt`) — defer until the harness has been used manually 3–5 times and we have enough run-to-run variance data to set sensible flake tolerances. Without that, automated runs would noise-flag every PR. (d) Reproducing the ADR-077 four-task fixture set inside this harness — those fixtures answered a different question (does codex catch real bugs?) on a different methodology (Claude builds a feature, differential probes injected into the implementation). ADR-100's fixtures answer "does the prompt change make codex flag the kind of bug it didn't flag before?" The two harnesses are complementary; ADR-100's fixtures are smaller, faster, and prompt-rule-specific. (e) Per-fixture rerunning under stochastic-variance averaging — codex is non-deterministic, but the harness runs each fixture once per arm. For high-stakes decisions, run the full benchmark 2–3 times and average the recall numbers; for routine prompt tweaks, one run is enough to spot large effects.
- **Consequences:** **What this gains**: (a) **Empirical validation path for prompt changes** that previously had only "well-articulated principles" as justification. ADR-099's value question becomes answerable in ~$0.40 of codex spend. (b) **Reusable harness**, not a one-off. Any future prompt change (severity-vs-urgency refactor, structured-output schema evolution, additional baseline rule, removal of an existing baseline rule) can vendor a new template snapshot and re-run against the same fixtures + decision rule. The harness *itself* is the durable artifact; ADR-099 becomes a use-case, not the primary work. (c) **Lived-experience discipline gets a measurement** — ADR-095's "shipping a tool with unmeasured outcomes is half the value" critique now has a concrete antidote for the prompt surface. Future contributors who want to change `prompts/review.txt` have a path that says "demonstrate the recall delta first." (d) **Fixture authoring is itself documentation** — each `probes.json` is a concrete statement of "this is the kind of bug we expect codex to catch." Adding fixtures forces precision about what codex-pair is *for*, which complements the task-agnostic ADR-098 framing by giving concrete examples of the rule classes. **What this costs**: (a) The harness ships in the plugin package and adds ~600 LOC across 6 scripts + 4 fixtures + 2 templates + 2 READMEs. The cost is mostly compile-time (TypeScript doesn't compile `.mjs`) and shipped-package-size (negligible — the benchmark dir is < 50KB). (b) Maintenance: the vendored `pre-baseline.txt` template snapshot will go stale if `prompts/review.txt` evolves further. Convention: when a new prompt change ships, vendor the new "post-change" snapshot alongside the existing "pre-change" snapshot and rename to reflect the change being measured (e.g. `templates/with-severity-refactor.txt` vs `templates/without.txt`). The harness's `--arms` argument (future enhancement) would parameterize this. (c) Keyword-based scoring is crude and will sometimes miss real matches (a finding with different vocabulary than the probe's keyword list) or false-positive match (vocabulary overlap that isn't semantic match). Mitigation: the report explicitly surfaces "extra findings" for human judgment, so the scoring isn't the final verdict — it's the first-pass filter. (d) Codex non-determinism means recall numbers vary run-to-run. The decision rule's +10 pp threshold is tuned to be larger than expected variance, but a borderline fixture (recall 60% vs 50%) shouldn't be treated as decisive on one run. **What this DOESN'T change**: the hook source, broker, cache, lock, parser, prompt rendering, anything in the runtime path — the harness is a standalone tool under `scripts/benchmark/` with no imports from the runtime layer (it has its own `render-prompt.mjs` that mirrors but doesn't share code with `lib/prompt.mjs`). This is intentional: keeps the benchmark from being a transitive dependency of the hook, and the harness can validate prompt changes that include changes to `lib/prompt.mjs` without bootstrap-circularity problems. **Plugin test count unchanged at 313** — no tests added for the benchmark scripts themselves (they're one-off maintainer tooling, not user-facing code, and over-testing one-off scripts is the kind of premature investment ADR-099's Simplicity baseline would flag). The benchmark scripts are exercised manually when run; lint covers their syntax via Biome. **Forward path**: (a) Run the benchmark against ADR-099's prompt change, record the result (recall delta + extra-finding delta), and either merge ADR-099 with the validating data appended or execute the rollback path. (b) Once the harness has 2–3 runs of operating experience, add an LLM-judge scoring mode for cases where keyword matching proves too crude. (c) Once 8–10 fixtures exist, consider promoting the harness to CI gated by a `[run-benchmark]` PR-title trigger so prompt changes can't ship without empirical justification. (d) The fixture format is intentionally similar to the ADR-077 four-task probe structure; if the ADR-077 fixtures get vendored from `experiment/codex-pair-poc`, this harness can run them too. Cross-validating the ADR-099 baseline against the original four-task corpus would be additional triangulation evidence.

## ADR-099: `codex-pair` Karpathy Baseline Principles in Review Prompt
- **Date:** 2026-05-26
- **Status:** Accepted (modifies `packages/claude-plugin/prompts/review.txt` template + the golden fixture per ADR-089; refines the review prompt every codex-pair fire renders; complements ADR-077 / ADR-083 / ADR-089)
- **Context:** Codex-pair's review prompt template (ADR-089) frames the reviewer as a "senior software engineer reviewing a file another AI agent just edited," with two dynamic blocks (`{{CONTEXT_BLOCK}}` for project marker content, `{{PARTIAL_VIEW_BLOCK}}` for over-cap files), grading criteria (HIGH / MED / LOW with definitions), and structured-output instructions. What the prompt did NOT have, before this ADR, was any explicit baseline articulation of *which categories of bug or anti-pattern the reviewer should look for* beyond "what's actually wrong or risky." Each project's marker file supplied domain-specific invariants (currency = integer cents, all routes check user.role, etc.), but absent any project-context content the reviewer was operating with only a generic "senior engineer" framing — which empirically produces inconsistent results across reviews because the model has no anchor for *what kind of issue counts as a finding* vs. ordinary code that happens to differ from how the model would have written it. The Karpathy CLAUDE.md (https://github.com/multica-ai/andrej-karpathy-skills/blob/main/CLAUDE.md) articulates four behavioral guidelines for LLM-coding agents: Think Before Coding, Simplicity First, Surgical Changes, Goal-Driven Execution. Three of these articulate properties evaluable on a code diff: code exceeding what the task requires (Simplicity), changes touching more than necessary (Surgical Changes), and implicit decisions that should have been surfaced (the diff-evaluable subset of Think Before Coding). The fourth (Goal-Driven Execution) is a metaprocess rule about how to approach a task; it has no diff-evaluation target and was intentionally not folded into the review prompt. **The integration question was whether to integrate the Karpathy guidelines as (A) a universal addition to the review prompt template, (B) a split — some rules into the prompt, some into CLAUDE.md as editing-agent behavior, or (C) a composable per-project baseline knob.** Option A was chosen: universal coverage, single integration point, lowest implementation friction. The cost surface (~360 tokens per review at ~$0.001/review overhead) is negligible against the existing $0.04–0.07/review codex spend; the cache surface is invalidated once per existing project on next edit after the upgrade lands, then back to normal. The decision NOT taken was Option B (split): in retrospect, while the diff-evaluable rules belong in codex-pair's prompt and the behavioral rules belong in CLAUDE.md, mixing those concerns into a single prompt was simpler than maintaining two surfaces. The behavioral subset can land in CLAUDE.md as a separate follow-on if value is empirically demonstrated.
- **Decision:** Insert a new `## Baseline review principles` section into `packages/claude-plugin/prompts/review.txt` BETWEEN the reviewer-role intro (line 1) and the dynamic `{{CONTEXT_BLOCK}}` substitution token. The section sits in front of project-specific marker content so the baseline is foundational; project context, when present, can override or refine baseline behavior via the marker's invariants. The section's content reframes three Karpathy-derived rules through the reviewer's perspective (each "Don't X when coding" becomes "Flag if the diff X" addressed to the reviewer): **(1) Simplicity** — flag code exceeding what the task requires (new features beyond what was asked; abstractions for single-use code; configurability/flexibility not requested; error handling for impossible scenarios; "200 lines where 50 would do"). **(2) Surgical scope** — flag changes touching more than necessary (modifications to adjacent code unrelated to the task; drive-by refactors mixed with substantive edits; comment/formatting changes mixed with logic; orphan imports/variables/functions; style drift from the file's existing conventions). **(3) Hidden assumptions** — flag implicit decisions that needed to be explicit (behavior dependent on an unstated invariant; a simpler alternative the diff didn't consider when one is obvious; multiple valid interpretations of the task with one silently picked). The section opens with a one-sentence framing ("These principles describe properties of healthy code changes. Treat violations as MED or HIGH findings unless a project-context rule below explicitly overrides them.") and an attribution line referencing the Karpathy source URL. **Out of scope explicitly**: (a) Rule 4 (Goal-Driven Execution) — has no diff-evaluation target; tracked for potential CLAUDE.md inclusion in a follow-on if value is empirically demonstrated. (b) Per-project opt-out via env var or frontmatter knob — Option A is "always on"; if a project's marker explicitly contradicts a baseline rule, the marker takes precedence (project-context override per the section's opening framing). (c) Empirical validation that the baseline actually improves recall or precision — the integration is justified by the Karpathy principles being well-articulated; a follow-on A/B benchmark (ADR-100 candidate) would measure recall/precision deltas vs. the pre-baseline prompt to confirm the change earns its keep. (d) Extending the baseline to ad-hoc `/codex-review` slash-command invocations — that surface uses a different prompt (precision-first, confidence ≥80); ADR-077 explicitly framed it as complementary, not competing. (e) Re-running the ADR-077 four-task benchmark with the new baseline included — would change the headline numbers (potentially upward or downward, depending on how the rules interact with the benchmark's specific probes); preserve the ADR-077 numbers as the pre-baseline baseline and treat ADR-099's effect as a separate measurement question.
- **Consequences:** **What this gains**: (a) **Consistent baseline across all opted-in projects** — every codex-pair fire applies the same simplicity / surgical-scope / hidden-assumption checks regardless of whether the project has a marker with specific domain invariants. New projects benefit immediately; existing projects pick up the baseline on their next edit after the plugin upgrades. (b) **Better signal-to-noise on routine bugs** — the bug class "code that compiles fine but does too much / touches too much / hides too much" is now an explicit review target rather than something the model might or might not flag depending on its mood. (c) **Reduced project-marker burden** — projects no longer need to spell out "don't refactor unrelated code" or "match existing style" in their marker; those are baseline. The marker can focus on actual domain invariants (currency, security, protocol conformance). (d) **Cost surface is small** — ~360 tokens per review at $0.04–0.07 means ~$0.0015 of overhead per fire. Cache invalidation is one-time per project on first edit after upgrade. **What this costs**: (a) Existing `.codex-pair/cache/` shards become stale for every opted-in project on the first edit after upgrade because the prompt content changes → the cache key (sha256 of model + prompt + content + threshold) changes → every cache lookup misses. Each project pays one extra ~$0.04–0.07 codex spawn per file on the first post-upgrade edit, then normal. (b) ADR-089's "byte-identical golden fixture" pin now references new content; future contributors who want to change the prompt template must update the golden together (existing process — no new burden, just a new line). (c) The baseline might over-flag on intentionally simple files where the existing code is naturally short and clean (a unit test file with 8 assertion lines might be flagged as "could be substantially simpler" if codex doesn't internalize that tests are intentionally explicit). Lived-experience monitoring will show whether this is a real noise source. (d) Adoption of the Karpathy framing carries an implicit endorsement of its philosophy; the attribution line acknowledges the source so contributors can trace the origin. **What this DOESN'T change**: ADR-077's silent-on-error contract, ADR-082's content-hash cache key shape (only the inputs to the hash change), ADR-087's inflight lock, ADR-091's atomicity, ADR-092's `.codex-pair/` layout, ADR-093's broker interface, ADR-096's repetition tracker, ADR-097's per-file sharding, ADR-098's `/codex-pair` user-invocable dashboard. The hook code (`codex-pair-watch.mjs`) is byte-identical; this is a prompt-only change. **Plugin test count unchanged at 313** — the existing ADR-089 golden-fixture test continues to pass because both template and fixture are updated together; the "no-whitespace-artifact" test (line 1902 of `codex-pair-watch.test.ts`) updated to assert the new baseline-block structure flows cleanly. Lint clean across 6 workspaces. **Empirical validation (added 2026-05-27 via ADR-100 harness run)**: full 4-fixture benchmark against `codex exec --json gpt-5.5` produced **recall delta +12.5 pp** (baseline 100% vs pre-baseline 87.5%) AND **extra-finding delta +0** (baseline added zero noise findings). Both decision-rule thresholds (≥+10pp recall, ≤+1/fixture extra) cleared. Fixture-by-fixture: 01-overcomplication baseline 100% vs pre-baseline 75% (+25 pp on Simplicity); 02-drive-by-refactor baseline 100% vs pre-baseline FAILED (codex hung — non-deterministic, sanity run had pre-baseline at 67%); 03 + 04 both arms tied at 100%. Zero fixtures where baseline regressed. Rollback path not exercised; ADR-099 stays in effect. **Forward path**: a follow-on ADR-100 should empirically benchmark recall/precision with the baseline vs. without, ideally on the same four-task fixture set ADR-077 used. If the baseline empirically improves recall, that's the validating evidence. If it doesn't, this ADR can be reversed in one PR (delete the baseline section from `prompts/review.txt`, restore the golden fixture, restore the "no-whitespace-artifact" test assertion to its pre-ADR-099 form). The reversal cost is two file edits and a test-assertion update.

## ADR-098: `codex-pair` Documentation Reframing + `/codex-pair` User-Invocable Dashboard
- **Date:** 2026-05-21
- **Status:** Accepted (rewrites positioning prose across SKILL.md + plugin/README.md + apps/docs/plugin/hooks.md + apps/docs/plugin/skills.md + apps/docs/plugin/overview.md; flips `codex-pair/SKILL.md` from `user_invocable: false` to `true` and adds a Phase 1–5 orchestration block; no underlying script changes; complements ADR-077 / ADR-095 / ADR-096 / ADR-097)
- **Context:** Two distinct UX failures surfaced from real-flow use of codex-pair, both pointing at the documentation rather than the mechanism. **(1) Example-as-fact confusion in LLM-consumed prose**: every documentation surface that described codex-pair's value did so by quoting the ADR-077 four-task benchmark's specific probe domains — "float-money precision, validation bypass, edge-case clamping" — as bare nouns inside sentences like "Use codex-pair when handling money / security-sensitive code." A human reader correctly parsed "these are examples of the kind of bug codex-pair catches in OTHER projects." An LLM reading the ask-llm codebase as project context for a review/brainstorm/codex-pair-on-itself task instead correctly parsed "ask-llm has money handling and security-sensitive auth paths" — because the docs presented those phrases as defining properties of when codex-pair earns its keep, not as benchmark fixtures. ask-llm is a CLI bridge between MCP clients and LLM CLIs; it has zero money code, zero auth, zero user-facing security surface. The mismatch caused codex-pair (and reviewer agents reading the corpus) to start hallucinating concerns about code that doesn't exist. The deeper learning: documentation written for humans-with-context generalizes poorly to LLMs-without-context, especially when the example domains used to illustrate a feature happen to be ones the consuming LLM might confuse with the project being documented. The fix is to be explicit about the abstraction level — the property codex-pair detects is "looks fine but runs wrong" (a *class* of bug), and the benchmark used four structurally different *fixtures* to demonstrate the class-detection holds task-agnostically. The improvement is task-agnostic per the empirical evidence; the docs were describing it as if it were domain-specific. **(2) No human-facing entrypoint to set up or check codex-pair**: the only ways to interact with codex-pair as a user were (a) manually create `.codex-pair/context.md` (requires reading the SKILL.md or README first, knowing what to put in it, and remembering to gitignore the directory), (b) invoke `/codex-pair-pause` / `/codex-pair-resume` (which assume codex-pair is already set up), (c) tail `.codex-pair/log.jsonl` to see what reviews ran (requires knowing the layout exists). There was no `is codex-pair currently running on this project?` answerable in one slash command, and no `set me up on this project` flow that auto-detects context from `README.md` + `package.json` rather than asking the user to write boilerplate from a template they haven't read. The `codex-pair` skill itself was `user_invocable: false` because it documented the hook BEHAVIOR (running automatically per edit) rather than presenting a user surface, which was structurally correct but left a UX gap.
- **Decision:** Two coupled changes shipped together. **(1) Task-agnostic re-framing of codex-pair positioning across 5 documentation surfaces** (plugin SKILL.md, plugin/README.md, apps/docs/plugin/hooks.md, apps/docs/plugin/skills.md, apps/docs/plugin/overview.md): the phrasing "money / security-sensitive paths / auth" is replaced everywhere with code-characteristic language ("code with hidden invariants the model can't infer from one file", "code where latent bugs cost more than per-edit review", "code evolving fast under written constraints", "state coordination / concurrency / order-sensitive logic"). The benchmark recap quotes the bug *class* ("looks fine, runs wrong") instead of probe-specific domain nouns. Every empirical claim now explicitly attributes the recall improvement as **task-agnostic** — measured across four structurally different fixtures (todo CRUD, URL shortener, RFC-spec implementation, stateful business logic), not just one. Each surface now includes an explicit "ask-llm itself is a CLI/MCP bridge with none of these properties; codex-pair runs against this repo for dogfooding" disclaimer where the surface is in the LLM-readable corpus, so future reviews of ask-llm don't confuse benchmark-fixture domains with project properties. The empirical numbers (2/10 → 7/10 → 10/10) are preserved verbatim — they're the load-bearing evidence; only the surrounding framing changes. ADR-077 and ADR-095 cross-references added to anchor the empirical basis at every claim site. **(2) `codex-pair/SKILL.md` flipped to `user_invocable: true` with a Phase 1–5 orchestration block at the top.** The skill body now begins with five numbered phases that Claude executes when `/codex-pair` is invoked: Phase 1 detects current state (walk for marker, check pause sentinel, tail recent log entries); Phase 2 branches on state (no marker → setup, marker+sentinel → paused dashboard, marker only → active dashboard); Phase 3 (setup) auto-detects project context by reading `README.md` + `package.json` + alternative manifests (`pyproject.toml` / `Cargo.toml` / `go.mod`), drafts a `.codex-pair/context.md` with a project-purpose summary + 3-5 inferred domain invariants, uses `AskUserQuestion` with the draft as the recommended option's `preview` field (so the user sees the content before deciding), confirms before writing, and ASKS rather than silently modifies `.gitignore`. Phase 4 (paused dashboard) renders a structured status table with paused-since timestamp + resume instruction. Phase 5 (active dashboard) renders a structured status table with marker model + surface threshold + cost-per-review + the last 5 reviews summary + active ignore/include patterns + pause instruction. The existing hook reference documentation (when-to-enable table, cost characteristics, output format, configuration knobs, empirical justification) moves below the orchestration block but is unchanged in substance — it serves as Claude's reference for explaining behavior to users mid-orchestration. **Out of scope explicitly**: (a) Adding a separate `codex-pair-status` or `codex-pair-setup` skill (would fragment the codex-pair concept across multiple files; the existing single SKILL.md absorbing both reference docs and orchestration is one less file to maintain). (b) Migrating existing `.codex-pair/context.md` markers — there's nothing to migrate; the new skill is additive. (c) Implementing the `/codex-pair` orchestration in a `.mjs` script — the entire flow uses Claude's existing tool surface (Bash, Read, AskUserQuestion) so no new code under `scripts/` ships; lower blast radius, no new build step. (d) Updating other surfaces that quote the ADR-077 numbers historically (DECISIONS.md ADR entries, ROADMAP.md "Recently shipped" historical bullets) — those are records of past decisions; rewriting them would falsify the historical narrative.
- **Consequences:** **What this gains**: (a) **LLM-corpus correctness** — codex-pair, reviewer agents, and any future LLM reading the ask-llm codebase as project context no longer hallucinate concerns about money/auth code that doesn't exist. The "is this an example or a fact about the project?" disambiguation is now explicit on every surface a corpus-indexer would see. (b) **Task-agnostic positioning matches empirical evidence** — the ADR-077 benchmark genuinely was structured to demonstrate task-agnosticism (four structurally different fixtures, identical methodology), and the docs now reflect that, broadening the credible audience for codex-pair from "payments/auth/security teams" (a misleadingly narrow framing) to "anyone with implicit correctness invariants in their code." (c) **One-command setup + status** — `/codex-pair` is now a self-contained entrypoint: a new user invokes it, gets auto-detected context, confirms, and is running in under a minute. No need to remember marker-file syntax, gitignore convention, or pause/resume mechanics; the skill handles everything via existing Claude tools. (d) **The skill file consolidates two concerns elegantly** — reference docs for the hook (which Claude reads when explaining behavior) and orchestration for the slash command (which Claude reads when executing it) coexist in one file with clear separation. **What this costs**: (a) Increase in `codex-pair/SKILL.md` size from ~140 to ~250 lines (the Phase 1-5 block + reconciliation language). Trade-off: one larger file vs. fragmenting across multiple skills. (b) The Phase 3 auto-detect-then-confirm flow takes ~30-60s of Claude wall-clock per invocation (file reads + drafting + AskUserQuestion round-trip). Acceptable for a one-time-per-project setup. (c) The "ask-llm itself contains no money/auth code" disclaimer adds ~30 words to multiple surfaces — durably helpful for LLM corpus consumers, mild redundancy for human readers who already knew. **What this DOESN'T change**: the hook script (`codex-pair-watch.mjs`), the broker scaffolding, the cache layout, the inflight-lock semantics, the marker-walk algorithm, the ADR-077 silent-on-error contract — all byte-identical to ADR-097. Existing `.codex-pair/` directories continue to work without re-setup. The `/codex-pair-pause` and `/codex-pair-resume` skills are unchanged. ADR-077's empirical claims hold; only the surrounding framing language changes. **Plugin test count unchanged at 313** — no new behavioral tests added because there's no new code to test (the orchestration runs through Claude's tool surface; structural pinning would over-couple the test to the skill's natural-language Phase descriptions). Lint clean across 6 workspaces. **Forward path**: the same pattern (slash-command-as-orchestration-layer over Claude's tool surface, with the underlying mechanism documented as reference in the same skill file) could absorb future codex-pair UX work — a `/codex-pair-status` would be redundant once `/codex-pair` does both setup and status; a future `/codex-pair-acknowledge <hash>` for the deferred per-finding ack feature could plug into the same skill as a Phase 6 branch.

## ADR-097: `codex-pair` UX Improvements — Multi-Review Hotfix on ADR-096 (Per-File Sharded Repetitions, TTL Sweep, Read-Only Cache-Hit Path, Negation-Only Include Semantics)
- **Date:** 2026-05-21
- **Status:** Accepted (hotfix on ADR-096; closes the four `/multi-review` findings noted explicitly as "tracked for a follow-on hotfix before wide adoption" in the v0.7.0 changeset; ships in v0.7.x patch line)
- **Context:** Immediately after ADR-096 landed (PR #107) and was bundled into the v0.7.0 changeset (PR #108), a fresh `/multi-review` of the merged ADR-096 diff surfaced four issues that both Gemini and Codex independently flagged (confidence 80+). The ADR-096 changeset itself called these out as known follow-ons. **(1) TOCTOU race on singleton `repetitions.json`** (Gemini conf 95, Codex conf 88): ADR-096's repetition tracker used a single project-level JSON file. Two `codex-pair-watch.mjs` processes editing different files concurrently each ran `loadRepetitions → mutate → saveRepetitions` against the same file; ADR-087's per-file inflight lock serializes same-file edits but does NOT cross-serialize across files, so the second writer's read could predate the first writer's write, dropping an increment. The bug is real but small in single-developer flow; under concurrent multi-edit (MultiEdit on related files, codex agentic mode, parallel agent runs) it would silently mis-count and delay the BLOCKING banner. **(2) Unbounded state growth** (Codex conf 85): repetition entries leaked for files that were once flagged then never re-edited. On a long-lived monorepo this would slowly grow the JSON file with stale entries for moved/deleted files. Bounded in practice by edit volume but not by time, so the ceiling is "however many distinct files have ever been flagged on this project." **(3) Cache-hit double-count under rapid re-saves** (Gemini conf 87): the ADR-096 `codex-pair-watch.mjs` called `updateRepetitions` on BOTH the live-review path AND the cache-hit path. Cache hits indicated "you re-saved the file with content identical to the last review" — which is exactly the "still ignoring" signal we wanted to amplify; but it also meant a rapid undo/redo cycle (Cmd+Z then Cmd+Shift+Z within 10min cache TTL) could push count from 1 → 3 → BLOCKING with NO new live review actually observing the concern again. The banner would fire on a false positive. **(4) Include-list negation-only edge case** (Codex conf 82): a `.codex-pair/include` containing only negation rules (e.g. only `!build/**`) would gate ALL files out — `matchesIgnoreRule` returns truthy only when SOME positive rule matches AND no negation cancels it; with no positive rule the gate fails for every file. The user's likely intent ("review everything except `build/`") would silently skip everything, the opposite of the intent. **The empirical-verification discipline (codified in ADR-095) was applied here too**: each finding was reproduced against actual code (sharding finding via tracing the cross-file read-modify-write window; cache-hit finding via a 10-line repro script; negation-only finding via single-edit test in a sandbox project; growth finding via inspection of cleanup paths) before deciding to fix. All four findings reproduced.
- **Decision:** Four targeted fixes shipped together as a single ADR-096 hotfix, with no breaking changes to the public state file format from a user perspective (the v1 singleton `repetitions.json` is silently dead-on-load — any v1 file lingering on disk is treated as empty state, no migration needed since the data is advisory and inherently regenerable from continued reviews). **(1) Per-file sharding** — repetitions state moves from `<markerDir>/.codex-pair/state/repetitions.json` (singleton) to `<markerDir>/.codex-pair/state/repetitions/<sha256(file)[0:16]>.json` (one shard per file, hashed with the same scheme ADR-087 uses for inflight locks). Schema bumps to `{ v: 2, file, entries: [{hash, count, firstSeenAt, lastSeenAt}] }`. Each shard's read-modify-write is now naturally serialized by ADR-087's per-file inflight lock (live-review path holds the lock for the full review duration including the post-review state update). Cross-file concurrency is no longer a problem because each file's state lives in a distinct path. The v1 singleton path is documented as dead in code comments; any existing v1 file is harmless (different path, ignored by the new code, won't be swept by the new TTL). **(2) TTL sweep** — new `sweepStaleRepetitions(markerDir)` drops shard files with mtime older than `REPETITIONS_TTL_MS` (30 days). Called probabilistically (5% per `updateRepetitions` call) so abandoned files don't accumulate state forever; amortizes O(N_files) cleanup cost across ~20 updates without needing a dedicated SessionStart hook. Best-effort, never throws — repetition tracking is advisory (ADR-077). **(3) Read-only cache-hit path** — new `getBlockingFromShard(markerDir, file, newHashes)` checks whether currently-cached concerns have already crossed the BLOCKING threshold WITHOUT incrementing or persisting. The cache-hit branch in `codex-pair-watch.mjs` now uses this function instead of `updateRepetitions`. Semantic: rapid identical-content re-saves still surface the banner if the count has previously crossed threshold from real live reviews, but they can't push a finding to threshold by themselves. Live reviews remain the only path that mutates state. **(4) Negation-only include transformation** — when `readIncludeFile(markerDir)` returns rules of which NONE are positive (i.e. all `!pattern` negations), the code transforms each `!pattern` into a positive ignore-list entry and proceeds with NO inclusion gate — effectively interpreting the include file as "review everything except these patterns" instead of "review nothing because there's no positive rule." A one-time info-level log entry per fire explains the semantic mapping so users see the behavior and can choose to add a positive `**` rule if they want strict-include semantics. The transformation re-uses the existing `matchesIgnoreRule` infrastructure — no new matching engine. **Backward-compat shims** — `loadRepetitions(markerDir)` and `saveRepetitions(markerDir, map)` are kept as no-op exports so any external scripts that imported the v1 surface don't break at import time; they don't operate on the new sharded layout and are documented as dead. **Out of scope explicitly**: (a) Migration of v1 `repetitions.json` data into v2 shards. Repetition state is advisory and continues to accumulate from future reviews; one missed cycle's increments are not worth a migration helper, since within 3 reviews of a still-ignored finding the new banner fires anyway. (b) Cross-process atomic claim of the inflight lock during the cache-hit path (cache hits don't acquire the lock today, per ADR-087; this hotfix does NOT change that — the read-only `getBlockingFromShard` is safe to call without the lock because it doesn't mutate). (c) Per-finding "acknowledged" persistence (still deferred — ADR-096 noted as next iteration). (d) Configurable TTL via env var (`CODEX_PAIR_REPETITIONS_TTL_MS`) — 30 days is generous; one config point not needed yet. (e) Migration log entry on first hit of the sweep — silent cleanup is fine.
- **Consequences:** **What this gains**: (a) The shard layout naturally inherits ADR-087's per-file serialization invariant — adding cross-file concurrency safety with zero new locking primitives. The fix uses an existing mechanism instead of inventing a new one. (b) The TTL sweep means the system has a self-stabilizing memory ceiling without needing a SessionStart cleanup hook (which would add coupling between the broker scaffolding and the repetitions feature). (c) The read-only cache-hit path means the BLOCKING banner is now only triggered by REAL repeated live reviews of an actually-flagged concern — the false-positive surface from undo/redo cycles is closed. (d) The negation-only include transformation matches user intent without breaking the strict-include semantics for users who do supply a positive rule. The info-level log line makes the behavior discoverable rather than mysterious. **What this costs**: (a) One extra directory inode per project that uses repetitions; the directory is small (one file per actively-flagged file, ~50-200 bytes each). (b) The probabilistic sweep means occasional updates do an extra readdir of the shards root; negligible relative to codex's 13-50s wall time. (c) Code-volume increase in `state.mjs` — five new exported helpers, ~80 LOC; complexity is contained behind the per-file API which is simpler than the singleton API was. **What this DOESN'T change**: every other ADR-077/081/082/085/086/087/088/092/093/094/095/096 contract holds byte-for-byte. The verdict closed-set, log JSONL shape, cache layout, inflight-lock semantics, broker interface, ADR-077 silent-on-error, `buildVerdictMessage` signature — all unchanged. The `.codex-pair/include` file format is unchanged from user perspective; the negation-only semantics only redefine what was previously a "skip everything silently" edge case. **Plugin test count 308 → 313** (+5 ADR-097 regression pins: per-file sharding API surface, TTL sweep correctness, read-only cache-hit non-mutation, threshold-crossing read-only surfacing, structural pin for negation-only branch). Lint clean across 6 workspaces. **Forward path**: with the four `/multi-review` findings closed, the next round of codex-pair UX work is the deferred severity-vs-urgency split (ADR-096 noted it as a breaking change to the prompt/parser; with repetition tracking proven out, escalation-via-repetition becomes a clean prototype of "urgency as a separate axis from severity"). The empirical pattern emerging from this hotfix — `/multi-review` immediately after every feature ADR catches issues the implementing author misses — is itself worth codifying as a discipline: ship a feature ADR, immediately run `/multi-review`, schedule a hotfix ADR if findings are real. ADR-095 captured this discipline for the consumer side; ADR-097 demonstrates its value on the developer side.

## ADR-096: `codex-pair` UX Improvements — Inclusion-List Scoping + Repetition Detector + Loud-Formatting for Repeated-Ignored Findings
- **Date:** 2026-05-21
- **Status:** Accepted (extends ADR-077, ADR-081, ADR-082, ADR-085, ADR-087, ADR-095; implements three concrete improvements identified in ADR-095's lived-experience feedback section)
- **Context:** ADR-095 captured an honest critique of codex-pair's UX after the Tier 3 broker work: the tool's findings are sharp, but in implementation flow the consumer (me) ignored 21 of 32 real findings because they piled up in `.codex-pair/log.jsonl` without surfacing loudly enough. The three highest-leverage suggestions from that critique: **(1)** repetition detector that promotes "you've ignored this 3+ times" to a BLOCKING tier, **(2)** loud-formatting for repeated-ignored findings (poor-man's STOPPER mode — Claude Code's PostToolUse hook can't actually block the next tool call, but bright formatting + ALL-CAPS banner makes the message un-scrollable-past in flow), **(3)** inclusion-list scoping so users can restrict codex-pair to high-stakes paths (e.g. `src/billing/**`, `src/auth/**`) instead of paying ~$0.05/edit on the whole repo. Excluded from this ADR (deferred): the full severity-vs-urgency refactor (would require a breaking change to the prompt template + parser), and a true platform-level STOPPER signal (Claude Code's PostToolUse hooks deliver `systemMessage` advisory-only — the platform doesn't currently support a "block the next tool call" return value at that event).
- **Decision:** Three composable improvements ship behind no env-flag (immediately active for every opted-in project), backward-compatible with all existing `.codex-pair/` semantics. **(1) Inclusion-list `.codex-pair/include`** mirrors the existing `.codex-pair/ignore` (ADR-081): gitignore-style globs, comments via `#`, negation via `!`. When the file exists AND has at least one non-comment rule, ONLY files matching at least one rule are reviewed; missing/empty = no scoping. The include gate runs BEFORE the ignore gate (include narrows the universe; ignore then excludes from the narrowed set). On non-match: silent log skip with `verdict:"skipped"` + reason `"file not in .codex-pair/include scope"` — same UX as ignore matches (no systemMessage, preserves silent-gating). Implementation re-uses the existing `globToRegex` + `matchesIgnoreRule` infrastructure via a shared `readGlobRulesFile(absolutePath)` helper that both `readIgnoreFile` and `readIncludeFile` delegate to. **(2) Repetition detector** lives at `<markerDir>/.codex-pair/state/repetitions.json` (versioned, `{v:1, entries:[{file, hash, count, firstSeenAt, lastSeenAt}]}`). On every concerns-verdict review of a file (cache-hit OR live), `updateRepetitions(markerDir, file, newHashes)` does: (a) for each prior entry on `file`, if its hash is in newHashes increment count + update lastSeenAt, else DROP from state (concern was likely fixed); (b) for each newHash not previously seen on `file`, insert with count=1; (c) save state atomically (tmp+rename per ADR-086); (d) return all entries with `count >= REPETITION_BLOCKING_THRESHOLD` (3). Reading is tolerant of missing/malformed/wrong-version files (empty Map). Failures are silent — repetition tracking is advisory and must never break the hook (ADR-077). **(3) Loud-formatting** in `parser.mjs::buildVerdictMessage`: accepts optional `repeatedIgnoredCount`. When `> 0`, prefixes the systemMessage with a multi-line banner using `🛑` markers, ALL-CAPS `REPEATED-IGNORED FINDING`, and language explicitly telling the consumer "please address the concerns below BEFORE continuing edits on this file." This is not a true STOPPER (the next tool call still proceeds), but the visual weight forces the consumer to actually see the finding instead of scrolling past. Both `buildVerdictMessage` call sites in `codex-pair-watch.mjs` (cache-hit path AND live-review path) pass `repeatedIgnoredCount` computed from the post-review `updateRepetitions` return value. Cache hits ALSO increment repetition counts because a cache hit on a concerns-verdict means the user re-edited the file without changing it — exactly the "still ignoring" signal we want to amplify. **Out of scope explicitly**: (a) Full severity-vs-urgency refactor (current HIGH/MED/LOW conflates both axes; redesigning is a breaking change to the prompt + parser + every cached entry — defer). (b) True platform STOPPER signal (Claude Code's PostToolUse hooks don't support a `decision: "block"` for the NEXT tool call — only for the JUST-RAN tool's stdout/stderr surface — and only PreToolUse hooks can block. Workaround: loud-formatting is the best PostToolUse can do today). (c) Inclusion-list with directory-only matching for monorepos (current implementation matches via the existing globToRegex which handles `**` correctly; complex monorepo patterns will need their own ADR if real-world use surfaces gaps). (d) Per-finding "acknowledged" persistence (would require a UI flow — `/codex-pair-ack <hash>` skill or similar; not in this ADR).
- **Consequences:** **What this gains**: (a) The 81% ignore-rate observed in ADR-095's M2 forensic audit should drop meaningfully because (i) repeated identical findings get a STOPPER-style banner the consumer can't visually skip, and (ii) inclusion-list lets users scope codex-pair to paths where ignoring a finding has higher cost (payment/auth/spec-implementing code) — the same code where ADR-077's recall-first design earns its keep. (b) The repetition tracker also provides a NEW observability signal: log analysis can now spot "user is ignoring concerns" patterns retroactively by reading `.codex-pair/state/repetitions.json` — `count` field tells you how many consecutive reviews surfaced each concern. (c) The inclusion-list reduces the per-edit cost surface for projects that want codex-pair only on a hot subset — moves the cost-vs-value tradeoff toward "leave it on" for more users. **What this costs**: (a) State file is per-project, ~50-200 bytes per tracked concern; bounded by REPETITION_BLOCKING_THRESHOLD (concerns either get fixed and drop, or hit threshold). (b) hash + update is O(n_concerns_per_review) — negligible relative to codex's 13-50s wall time. (c) The cache-hit path now does ONE extra fs.readFileSync (repetitions.json) per review; ~µs cost, dwarfed by everything else. **What this DOESN'T change**: every ADR-077/081/082/085/087/088/092/093/094/095 contract holds byte-for-byte. The verdict closed-set, log JSONL shape, cache layout, inflight-lock semantics, broker interface, ADR-077 silent-on-error — all unchanged. New tests: 8 new structural+behavioral pins (308 plugin tests after this PR vs 300 on ADR-093 M4 merge). **Forward path for ADR-097+**: the deferred severity-vs-urgency split is the natural next iteration. With repetition tracking in place, the system can identify "this concern has been flagged 3+ times, ESCALATE the urgency" as a separate axis from confidence-by-severity. That feels like the right shape for the next round of UX improvements, after empirical evidence accumulates on how often the BLOCKING banner gets ignored vs acted on.

## ADR-095: `codex-pair` Lived-Experience Lessons — Consumption-Pattern Failure Mode + Reviewer-Agent Calibration + M2 Debt Paydown
- **Date:** 2026-05-20
- **Status:** Accepted (lessons learned ADR from the May-20 broker work; codifies a behavioral discipline + ships 6 verified-real bug fixes flagged by codex-pair during M2 development; reviewer-agent calibration; complements ADR-077, ADR-090, ADR-093)
- **Context:** Across the May-20 session implementing Tier 3 Milestone 2 of the codex-pair broker (PRs #99, #100, #101, hotfix #103), codex-pair (the recall-first review hook this plugin ships) was firing on every Edit/Write/MultiEdit and writing findings to `.codex-pair/log.jsonl`. End-of-session forensic audit of the log (`node scripts/codex-pair-log.mjs --since 24h --summary` showing 120 entries / 44 verdict-concerns / 6 NONEs / 51 skipped / 50 uncached codex runs averaging 69.9s wall-clock) revealed a structural failure mode: **codex-pair caught 32 unique bugs across M2 development, of which 21 were ignored as I built increasingly-broken code on top of unaddressed findings.** Two of the 21 ignored findings were BLOCKING bugs — the "WebSocket handshake is never sent" finding at 12:15 PM and the "ESM `require` always returns 'unknown'" finding at 12:39 PM — that /multi-review independently re-discovered at ~5:30 PM. Codex-pair caught both bugs FIVE+ HOURS before /multi-review did, on the exact files where I was actively writing the broken code. The other 19 unaddressed findings were a mix of HIGH-severity (broker detach was-by-design but flagged anyway; PID-reuse hazard, lock-stomp race) and MED (server-initiated-request handling gap, control-frame size cap, etc.) — some real, some false positives where codex didn't know about the surrounding ADR-codified design. **The failure was on the consumer side, not the tool side.** Codex-pair's findings were sharp; my consumption pattern (running in implementation flow, not reading the log between turns) lost the signal. This ADR codifies: (a) which of the 21 ignored findings are verified-real bugs worth fixing (6); (b) which are by-design false-positives (1, with documented justification); (c) which are deferred-known-limitations (3, tracked); (d) the reviewer-agent calibration updates that make findings more consumable; (e) the consumption-pattern discipline I'll apply going forward. **The wider lesson generalizes**: any review-loop AI tool's value depends entirely on the consumer reading and acting on its findings. A high-quality reviewer surfacing 30 sharp findings into a sink the consumer doesn't read produces zero value. This ADR is about making findings consumable enough — and making the consumer disciplined enough — that the signal doesn't get lost. Codex-pair's empirical 4-task benchmark (Claude alone 2/10 → +/codex-review 7/10 → +codex-pair 10/10 from ADR-077) is real but conditional on consumption.
- **Decision:** Three shipping changes + one documented behavioral discipline. **(1) Six verified-real bug fixes** in `packages/claude-plugin/scripts/lib/broker-lifecycle.mjs` and `broker-transport.mjs`, each empirically traced to a reproducible failure mode before fixing (per the user's explicit "verify before fixing" directive — codex-pair findings deserve the same skepticism as any LLM output, not blind trust): (a) `sleep()` helper no longer calls `.unref()` on its timer — the previous code let Node exit during the await if no other ref-holding handle was alive, orphaning the partially-spawned broker in `pollSocketReachable`'s backoff loop. (b) `spawnBroker` now attaches a `child.on("error", ...)` listener — `spawn("codex", ...)` emits ENOENT asynchronously when codex isn't on PATH; without a listener, the unhandled error crashes the hook process despite ADR-077's silent-on-error contract. The new no-op listener routes the failure through `bootstrapBroker`'s outer catch instead. (c) `bootstrapBroker` budget enforcement replaces `Math.max(100, ...)` and `Math.max(500, ...)` floors with strict `if (budget <= 0) throw` deadline checks — the floors let bootstrap overshoot the documented 5000ms wall-clock budget by up to 600ms. The new code fails fast at exhaustion and falls back to per-edit spawn (per ADR-077). (d) Descriptor write uses the imported `BROKER_PROTOCOL_VERSION` constant instead of a hardcoded `"v2"` string — the previous code had a literal "v2" with a comment acknowledging the drift risk (if the constant bumps to "v3" but the string doesn't, stale-recovery would treat every fresh broker as live and skip re-spawn). (e) `bootstrapBroker` catch block now closes the bootstrap RPC connection — previously a `writeBrokerDescriptor` failure (EACCES, disk full) would skip the `connection.close()` (placed after the descriptor write in the success path) and leak the connection. `connection` is now hoisted and closed in the catch with a 1011 code. (f) `clearStaleBrokerState` transport-scheme dispatch — previously treated `extractSafeSocketPath === null` as "we don't know, assume live" because `socketOk` defaulted to true. The new logic distinguishes: `unix://`-inside-bounds → check socket file; `unix://`-outside-bounds → STALE (tampered descriptor); `ws://` → assume live (per-edit probe validates); unknown scheme → STALE. **(2) One documented false-positive** with rationale in code comments: codex-pair flagged `child.unref()` in `spawnBroker` as HIGH severity, but this is BY DESIGN per ADR-090's broker-as-session-scoped-daemon lifecycle. The comment + the new codex-reviewer.md guidance to check ADRs before flagging are the durable safeguards against re-flagging by codex's pattern matcher. **(3) Reviewer-agent calibration** in `packages/claude-plugin/agents/codex-reviewer.md`: severity-first reporting (BLOCKING/IMPORTANT/ADVISORY in the report ORDER, not just labels); mandatory reproduction paths for BLOCKING findings; ADR-aware false-positive filtering (Phase 4 step 5 explicitly drops patterns documented as intentional); anti-noise heuristics (no re-flagging same root cause across files; no padding confidence to meet threshold; escalate REPEATED-IGNORED findings with a "REPEATED FINDING — consider this BLOCKING" prefix). The agent file's "Calibration" section quotes this ADR's lived-experience numbers (32 bugs found, 21 ignored, 2 BLOCKING that multi-review re-caught hours later) so future reviewer-tool maintainers see the failure-mode data, not just the rules. **(4) Behavioral discipline** I'm adopting going forward (and recommending for any other AI assistant consuming codex-pair in flow): tail `.codex-pair/log.jsonl` between bursts of edits (not just at milestone boundaries); treat HIGH-severity findings as immediately-blocking; treat MED as PR-blocker checklist items; use `codex-pair-log.mjs --summary --since 30m` as a between-sub-PR review checkpoint. **Deliberate scope limits** (out of this PR): (a) the 3 deferred-known-limitations from /multi-review on M2 (SessionEnd lock-stomp Gemini #3, PID-reuse hazard Codex #5, fixture-pid 999999 test-quality concern) remain tracked in ROADMAP — they're real but require larger refactors (fingerprint-beyond-pid; cross-session coordination). (b) The broker `outputSchema` ↔ `lib/parser.mjs` contract mismatch flagged at 12:26 PM is an M3 transition concern, not an M2 bug — M3's submitReview implementation will harmonize the contracts. (c) The "fragmentation handler doesn't terminate connection" finding from a later codex-pair flag was already partially addressed in PR #103 (parser destroys socket on fatal); the remaining "Frames buffered after upgrade can be delivered before listeners attach" is a real race but low-impact in practice (initialize is awaited before the consumer attaches its message listener); deferred to a follow-on.
- **Consequences:** The 6 verified fixes close real failure modes that were SHIPPED on `main` after PR #103 — they were latent for hours and would have produced field bugs under real `codex app-server` integration (M4's end-to-end test). **What this gains beyond the fixes themselves**: (a) The "documented behavioral discipline" point #4 is the most important durable outcome — without it, codex-pair's next milestone of findings will be ignored too. The discipline isn't enforceable by code; it's a habit that depends on the consumer (human or AI) actually reading the log. ADR-077's original empirical claim (2/10 → 7/10 → 10/10) was implicitly conditional on consumption; this ADR makes the condition explicit. (b) The reviewer-agent calibration in `codex-reviewer.md` (severity-first, reproduction-required, ADR-aware) makes findings more consumable. **What this DOESN'T do**: it doesn't fix the fundamental UX gap that codex-pair's `systemMessage` field isn't always delivered to the consumer in time. The hook writes findings to a file; the consumer reads the file. There's no push-notification channel that interrupts the consumer mid-flow when a HIGH finding lands. That's a future enhancement (Tier 3 follow-on idea — a "blocking notification" mode that pauses the consumer's next tool call until they acknowledge new HIGH findings) but explicitly out of this ADR's scope. **Plugin test count 271 → 278** (+7 regression pins for the 6 fixes — structural source-grep pins guard against re-introduction). Lint clean across 6 workspaces. **Reproducibility note for future ADR-style "lessons learned" entries**: this ADR was written by reading `.codex-pair/log.jsonl` end-to-end via `node scripts/codex-pair-log.mjs --summary --since 24h` + manual line-by-line trace of each flagged finding against current source. The discipline I'm prescribing for the future is exactly the audit method I used here. The full unprocessed log entry IDs and timestamps are captured in the assistant transcript and the `.codex-pair/log.jsonl` archive; no separate forensic file was created. **The deepest lesson, stated plainly**: a recall-first reviewer is only as valuable as the consumer's attention. ADR-077 promised 5x recall improvement; that promise held empirically in M2, but only on the bugs the consumer eventually noticed (with multi-review's help, hours late). For the same tool to deliver 5x continuously, consumption discipline matters more than the tool's prompt quality. This ADR's behavioral changes (point #4) are what make the tool earn its keep continuously — and they're the part that requires explicit codification because they're behavioral, not mechanical.

## ADR-094: Remove PreToolUse Pre-Commit Gemini-Review Hook (Codex-Pair + `/gemini-review` Skill Cover the Same Need with Better Recall and Better UX)
- **Date:** 2026-05-20
- **Status:** Accepted (deprecates `packages/claude-plugin/scripts/pre-commit-review.sh`, removes the `PreToolUse` registration from `hooks/hooks.json`, deletes `src/__tests__/pre-commit-script.test.ts`; v0.7.x cleanup)
- **Context:** The plugin shipped a `PreToolUse` hook (matcher: `Bash`, action: detect `git commit` substring, pipe staged diff to `gemini -p` for a quick advisory review) since the early plugin work. The hook was always **advisory-only** — it warned about issues but never blocked commits, by design. Three forces pushed toward removal now: **(1) Codex-pair delivers strictly more value continuously.** Since ADR-077 shipped, every Edit/Write/MultiEdit gets a Codex review against the project's `.codex-pair/context.md` invariants — HIGH and MED concerns surface to Claude on the next turn, LOW concerns log silently. The pre-commit hook reviewed the SAME staged diff that Claude had just produced via Edit/Write, except 30+ seconds later, with a less powerful prompt, against no project context, with no severity grading, with no structured verdict. **It was a strictly worse signal than codex-pair on every dimension.** The empirical 2/10 → 7/10 → 10/10 benchmark from ADR-077 used per-file review; a coarser commit-time review can only do worse. **(2) `/gemini-review` covers the explicit-review need.** The user-invocable `/gemini-review` slash command runs the same Gemini-CLI review surface on demand — but with control over WHEN it runs (you ask before commit when you want it, not on every git operation), WHAT it reviews (you pipe any diff scope you want, not just `--cached`), and how (with confidence filtering via the reviewer subagent). The pre-commit hook was a less-flexible, always-on version of what the skill already provides on-demand. **(3) The hook caused real operational friction.** Every Bash invocation that contained `git commit` paid a shell-spawn + diff-extraction tax (and a Gemini-CLI invocation if a diff existed). On a 50-commit session this added 30-60 minutes of cumulative latency for review output users rarely read because they'd already seen codex-pair's findings during editing. Worse: the hook fired on EVERY Bash call (not just `git commit`) — the `git commit` filter was in the script body, after Claude Code dispatched to the hook. The exit-0 short-circuit was fast, but the dispatch-and-skip cost was paid on every Bash. **What else was considered**: (a) reusing the hook but switching the provider from Gemini to Codex — rejected, since codex-pair already does codex review with better signal and the commit-time review still adds latency. (b) gating the hook behind an env var (`ASK_LLM_PRECOMMIT=1`) — rejected as half-measure complexity; the right answer is "use the on-demand skill". (c) replacing the bash script with a Node script — rejected because the script logic isn't the problem; the hook category is. (d) keeping the hook for "users who haven't opted into codex-pair" — rejected because those users can opt into codex-pair in 5 seconds (`mkdir -p .codex-pair && cat > .codex-pair/context.md`), AND because the pre-commit hook fires unconditionally for everyone whether they want it or not.
- **Decision:** Delete the hook end-to-end. **(1) Source removal**: `packages/claude-plugin/scripts/pre-commit-review.sh` deleted. The script's logic (filter Bash for `git commit`, get staged diff with sensitive-file exclusions, truncate to 50KB, pipe to `gemini -p @tempfile` with timeout guard + trap cleanup, exit 0) is preserved in git history (`ADR-040` codified the trap; `ADR-067` adjusted the staged-diff handling) but no longer ships in v0.7.x+. **(2) Registration removal**: `packages/claude-plugin/hooks/hooks.json` drops its `PreToolUse` block; the file now only registers `PostToolUse` (codex-pair-watch), `SessionStart`, and `SessionEnd` (broker scaffolding per ADR-090 + ADR-093). **(3) Test removal**: `packages/claude-plugin/src/__tests__/pre-commit-script.test.ts` deleted in full (no consumers; covered the deleted script). **(4) Test refactor**: `src/__tests__/manifest.test.ts`'s `hooks.json` describe block rewritten to assert NEW invariants: (a) `Stop` hook is NOT present (existing — ADR-048), (b) `PreToolUse` hook is NOT present (new — this ADR), (c) `PostToolUse` codex-pair declared, (d) `SessionStart`/`SessionEnd` declared, (e) all commands reference `${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}` for portability, (f) referenced script files exist (`codex-pair-watch.mjs` + `codex-pair-session.mjs`), (g) `pre-commit-review.sh` does NOT exist. **(5) Docs updates**: removal-noted in `apps/docs/plugin/hooks.md` (full pre-commit section gone + intro updated to "three hooks: PostToolUse + SessionStart + SessionEnd"); `apps/docs/plugin/overview.md` (Hooks table row removed + frontmatter updated + "Both hooks shell out" paragraph rewritten); top-level `README.md` (Hooks table row replaced with codex-pair entry); `apps/docs/installation.md` + `apps/docs/resources/faq.md` + `apps/docs/.vitepress/components/SetupTabs.vue` (mentions of "pre-commit hook" updated to "continuous codex-pair review hook"); `apps/docs/resources/troubleshooting.md` (entire pre-commit troubleshooting modal deleted — non-applicable). **(6) Migration story for existing users**: zero migration required. Users who had the hook installed got passive review on `git commit`; that goes away on the next plugin update. The on-demand `/gemini-review` skill is available immediately as the replacement surface — `gemini-cli` is the same dependency the hook used, so users with the hook working have `/gemini-review` working. Users with codex-pair opted in (project marker present) get a stricter, recall-first per-file review continuously — better than the pre-commit hook provided. **Out of scope**: (a) keeping a deprecated stub hook that no-ops; the registration removal is clean. (b) telemetry on how often the pre-commit hook surfaced concerns vs `/gemini-review`; not collected, no rollback signal needed. (c) cross-tool symbiosis ideas (codex-pair feeds findings to a future commit-time aggregator); pure removal, not refactor.
- **Consequences:** **What this gains**: (a) **Faster Bash commands**. Every `git commit` no longer pays the hook-dispatch + shell-spawn + diff-extraction cost. Users who didn't read the advisory output get back ~5-30s per commit. (b) **Cleaner mental model.** "What hooks does this plugin install?" is now answerable in one sentence: one PostToolUse codex-pair hook (opt-in per project), plus SessionStart/SessionEnd lifecycle scaffolding for the broker. Easier to onboard new contributors; easier to reason about plugin behavior. (c) **Single source of review signal.** codex-pair is the codex-pair plugin's review primitive; `/gemini-review` is the on-demand skill. No third surface that was strictly worse than both. (d) **Smaller install surface.** One fewer script to ship via `git-subdir`, one fewer test file, one fewer ADR-040-style trap-cleanup invariant to maintain. **What this DOESN'T break**: codex-pair's PostToolUse hook is unchanged; the SessionStart/SessionEnd broker scaffolding is unchanged; the `.codex-pair/` directory layout (ADR-092) is unchanged; the broker interface (ADR-090 + ADR-093) is unchanged. **What users will notice**: (a) `git commit` runs are quieter (no Gemini stderr review output appearing post-commit). (b) The "Hooks" docs page is shorter and easier to scan. (c) If a user explicitly relied on the advisory output (rare, since it was post-commit-anyway), they switch to running `git diff --cached | ask-gemini-run "review these staged changes"` or `/gemini-review` before committing — both are documented in the README. **Reversal cost if we were wrong**: trivial. The hook script + registration + test file are recoverable from git history; restoring would be one PR. Codex-pair has been live for months and the precedent (PRs #92, #93, #95, #97 in May 2026) shows the plugin can evolve cleanly via small, reversible PRs. **What this DOES require going forward**: when codex-pair Tier 3 (broker, ADR-090 + ADR-093 milestones 2-4) ships, the SessionStart/SessionEnd registrations remain unchanged from this ADR's hooks.json shape. Future hooks (broker health-check helpers, etc.) ADD new event registrations; they don't restore the PreToolUse block.

## ADR-093: `codex-pair` Broker — Protocol Discovery (`thread/start` + `turn/start` with `ephemeral` + `outputSchema`, Not `review/start`)
- **Date:** 2026-05-20
- **Status:** Accepted (refines ADR-090; v0.7.2 Tier 3 milestone 1 of 4; gates Tier 3 implementation milestones 2–4)
- **Context:** ADR-090 shipped the broker INTERFACE (`lib/broker.mjs` exports `submitReview`, `probeBrokerHealth`, `isBrokerEnabled`, etc.) with stubs, deferring implementation to Tier 3 follow-on work. The original ADR assumed we would design a custom `submitReview` JSON-RPC method against `codex app-server` — but didn't probe codex's actual protocol surface to confirm what primitives the server speaks. This created design risk: the broker.mjs interface might not map cleanly to whatever codex actually exposes. **Tier 3 milestone 1**: probe the real `codex app-server` (codex-cli 0.130.0) via `codex app-server generate-json-schema` and document the actual protocol surface, then refine the broker.mjs interface to match. Without this milestone, the implementation milestones (2–4) would build against assumptions; with it, they build against the documented contract. **What changed in our understanding of the protocol**: (1) codex ships a SCHEMA GENERATOR (`codex app-server generate-json-schema --out <dir>`) and a TS BINDINGS GENERATOR (`codex app-server generate-ts`) — the JSON-RPC contract is published by the binary itself, not reverse-engineered. (2) There are FOUR transport modes documented in `--listen`: `stdio://`, `unix://`, `unix://PATH`, `ws://IP:PORT`, plus `off`. ADR-090's POSIX-unix-socket / Windows-WebSocket split is correct. (3) The protocol exposes **75 client-callable methods** — far richer than ADR-090 anticipated. (4) Critically, there IS a `review/start` method, but **its review targets are git-changeset-based** (`uncommittedChanges`, `baseBranch`, `commit`) — designed for PR-style review of git changes, NOT file-content review of transient unsaved state. codex-pair reviews files DURING editing (may not be `git add`'d), so `review/start` is the wrong primitive. (5) The CORRECT primitive pair for codex-pair is `thread/start` + `turn/start`: start a fresh ephemeral thread, send the review prompt as a turn, listen for `turn/completed`. (6) `thread/start` supports `ephemeral: true` — natively discards the thread after the turn, achieving recall-first isolation at the PROTOCOL level rather than via application-level workarounds. (7) `turn/start` supports `outputSchema: <JSON Schema>` — codex constrains the agent's final message to the schema. This means our structured-verdict shape (HIGH/MED/LOW concerns arrays per ADR-083) can be enforced by the server, eliminating the prose-parsing concerns from `lib/parser.mjs`. (8) `turn/interrupt` provides first-class cancellation — exactly the primitive ADR-090 deferred.
