(9) The protocol is bidirectional JSON-RPC 2.0: server can send `ServerRequest` for human-in-the-loop approvals (`item/autoApprovalReview/*`, `commandExecApproval`, etc.) — codex-pair must configure `approvalPolicy: "never"` on `thread/start` so no approval-request blocks waiting for a user that doesn't exist in a hook context.
- **Decision:** Refine the Tier 3 design and the broker.mjs interface to match the real protocol surface. **(1) Transport detection unchanged** from ADR-090: POSIX → `unix://<markerDir>/.codex-pair/state/<BROKER_SOCKET_PREFIX>.<sha256(markerDir)[0:8]>.sock`, Windows → `ws://127.0.0.1:<random-port>`. **(2) Handshake flow**: SessionStart spawns `codex app-server --listen <transport>`, waits for ready-line on stdout/stderr (codex app-server emits a ready signal once the listener is bound), then opens a JSON-RPC connection and sends `initialize` with `clientInfo: { name: "codex-pair", version: "<plugin version>" }`. The descriptor file `<markerDir>/.codex-pair/state/broker.json` is written AFTER successful `initialize`, including PID, transport URL, codex version (from `codex --version`), protocol version (from `initialize` response). **(3) Per-review flow (the new `submitReview` semantics)** is no longer a single JSON-RPC call but a 3-step protocol dance: (a) `thread/start { ephemeral: true, cwd: <markerDir>, baseInstructions: <context.md content>, model: <config.model>, approvalPolicy: "never", sandbox: <readonly recommended> }` — receives `{ thread: { id } }` back. (b) `turn/start { threadId: <id>, input: [{ type: "text", text: <review prompt + file content> }], outputSchema: <verdict-shape JSON schema mirroring ADR-083's structured contract>, effort: "high" }` — receives `{ turn: { id } }` back. (c) Listen for `turn/completed` notification, parse its terminal `agentMessage` into the structured verdict, return same shape `spawnCodex` returns today (preserves caller-API stability for ADR-077's hook flow). **(4) Cancellation contract**: if the hook's parent process gets SIGINT/SIGTERM mid-review, send `turn/interrupt { turnId }` to the broker. The inflight-lock helper (ADR-087) already tracks per-file in-flight state; the broker integration extends this to also send the interrupt. **(5) Health probe**: `probeBrokerHealth(state)` sends an empty `initialize` (or a `model/list` — cheaper) request with a 2s timeout (`BROKER_HEALTH_TIMEOUT_MS` from ADR-090). Healthy = response received within deadline; otherwise treat as stale. **(6) Approval-policy hardening**: ALL `thread/start` requests from codex-pair MUST set `approvalPolicy: "never"`. If a server request (`commandExecApproval`, `applyPatchApproval`, etc.) arrives despite that policy (defensive — protocol bug or model misbehavior), the broker client MUST respond with a rejection rather than blocking. Codex-pair is a read-only reviewer; the model should never be requesting destructive actions, but defense-in-depth matters. **(7) `outputSchema` shape**: mirror ADR-083's structured verdict contract — `{ type: "object", required: ["verdict", "concerns"], properties: { verdict: { enum: ["none", "concerns"] }, concerns: { type: "object", properties: { high: { type: "array", items: { type: "string" } }, med: { ... }, low: { ... } } } } }`. Server enforces; parser becomes a simple JSON.parse. **(8) broker.mjs interface refinement** ships in this PR (no implementation, just interface): `submitReview` signature gains `{ baseInstructions, prompt, model, threadOptions, outputSchema, abortSignal }` shape (was: `(state, prompt, options)`). The new shape makes the JSON-RPC mapping explicit. The function body still throws "not implemented yet" — implementation lands in Milestone 3. **(9) New helper `buildVerdictSchema()` in `lib/broker.mjs`** — returns the JSON Schema for `outputSchema` arg. Centralized so test fixtures match what production sends. **What this DOESN'T change**: every consumer of `spawnCodex` in `codex-pair-watch.mjs` keeps the same return shape (the broker integration adapts internally). The cache, log JSONL shape, inflight lock, pause sentinel, marker gate, ADR-077 silent-on-error contract — all unchanged. The `ASK_CODEX_BROKER=1` gate (per ADR-090) still defaults off; production behavior is byte-identical. **Out of scope** (deferred to subsequent Tier 3 milestones): (a) Milestone 2: SessionStart spawn + initialize handshake + descriptor write + SessionEnd teardown. (b) Milestone 3: `thread/start` + `turn/start` + notification listener + verdict extraction (the heart of `submitReview`). (c) Milestone 4: integration call in `codex-pair-watch.mjs`'s main() between marker resolution and cache check + stale-broker recovery + version-skew detection + end-to-end test with a real `codex app-server` spawn.
- **Consequences:** The Tier 3 implementation work is now lower-risk because we have ground-truth contracts: the JSON-RPC method names, params shapes, notification stream, ephemeral-thread support, and outputSchema enforcement are all documented by the codex binary itself via `generate-json-schema`. **What this gains**: (a) Compile-time confidence — the schema generator's output can be checked into CI as a fixture, and any future codex protocol change that breaks our usage gets caught by schema-diff. (b) The `outputSchema` finding eliminates a class of bugs codex-pair has had to defend against (free-text concerns that don't parse) — server enforces the shape. (c) The `ephemeral: true` finding aligns recall-first design with the protocol's first-class primitive instead of working around the protocol. (d) Cancellation (`turn/interrupt`) is no longer a Tier 3+ research item — the protocol gives it to us for free. (e) The 75-method surface (including `fs/*`, `command/exec/*`, `mcpServer/*` etc.) opens future enhancement avenues but is OUT OF SCOPE for codex-pair, which only needs `initialize`, `thread/start`, `turn/start`, `turn/interrupt`, and the `turn/completed` notification. **What this costs**: (a) ADR-090's `submitReview(state, prompt, options)` signature is being refined to match the real protocol — anyone who was about to implement against the old signature has to read this ADR. Mitigation: the old signature throws "not implemented yet" today, so no consumer exists. (b) The output-schema strictness might cause some review failures if codex's model can't always produce valid JSON for the schema (rare, but possible on complex code). Mitigation: ADR-077's silent-on-error contract holds — a schema-violation falls back to the per-edit `spawnCodex` path. (c) One extra round-trip per review (thread/start AND turn/start vs a single `review/start`) — but the alternative was the wrong abstraction (git-changeset-based). The ms-level extra latency is acceptable against the much-larger structural fit. **Forward path for milestones 2–4 (each shippable independently, each behind `ASK_CODEX_BROKER=1` flag)**: Milestone 2 ships `spawn` + handshake + descriptor write; broker is reachable but no review path uses it. Milestone 3 ships the full JSON-RPC client; broker can submit reviews and parse turn/completed but the hook doesn't call it. Milestone 4 wires the hook (one-line `if (isBrokerEnabled(markerDir)) { ... }` between marker resolution and cache check) and ships the end-to-end test that spawns a real `codex app-server`, sends `submitReview`, and asserts the verdict shape. **Reference artifacts**: the JSON Schema bundle from `codex app-server generate-json-schema --out <dir>` (490KB, includes v1 and v2 protocol variants) should be checked in as a CI fixture under `packages/claude-plugin/src/__tests__/_fixtures/codex-app-server-schema/` in Milestone 2 — protects against silent codex CLI protocol changes by failing tests when the schema drifts.

## ADR-092: `codex-pair` Hook — Unified `.codex-pair/` Directory Layout (Marker, Log, Cache, Ignore, State All Nested)
- **Date:** 2026-05-19
- **Status:** Accepted (extends ADR-077, ADR-079, ADR-081, ADR-082, ADR-085, ADR-086, ADR-087, ADR-088, ADR-090, ADR-091; v0.7.1; no migration helper — per the maintainer's explicit instruction "I am only the user so far, I will move all manually" + "Keep the code clean")
- **Context:** Prior to this ADR, the codex-pair hook scattered its state across five sibling-flat paths in the project root: `.codex-pair-context.md` (marker, ADR-077), `.codex-pair-log.jsonl` (log, ADR-079), `.codex-pair-ignore` (gitignore-style globs, ADR-081), `.codex-pair-cache/` (content-hash cache, ADR-082), `.codex-pair-state/` (pause sentinel from ADR-085 + inflight locks from ADR-087, with ADR-090 reserving `.codex-pair-state/broker.json` for the future app-server descriptor). The flat layout had three growing problems: **(1) gitignore enumeration** — every new state surface added a new top-level line; .gitignore had four codex-pair entries already and the broker (ADR-090) plus any Tier 3 follow-ons (health-probe markers, version-skew indicators, request-cancellation tokens) would each have demanded their own. **(2) Visual noise** — `ls -la` in an opted-in project showed five codex-pair entries scattered alphabetically among the project's real top-level files (a payment service's `src/`, `tests/`, `package.json`, `.env.example`, etc.); the artifacts looked like first-class project state rather than per-developer review machinery. **(3) Discoverability** — a contributor curious about what codex-pair stores had to grep five different patterns; there was no single "show me everything codex-pair persists" mental model. The reference design (`openai/codex-plugin-cc`) uses a single `.codex/` directory; this ADR adopts the same shape under the name `.codex-pair/` (the project's distinguishing prefix) so contributors familiar with the upstream pattern transfer the mental model immediately. **What this ADR is NOT**: not a behavior change. Every hook contract — silent-on-error, marker-gate, HIGH/MED surface, LOW log, PIPE_BUF clamp, PID-scoped tmp from ADR-091, TOCTOU identity snapshot, UTF-8 byte accounting — is byte-for-byte unchanged. Cache keys still hash the same `(model, prompt, fileContent, surfaceThreshold)` tuple (cache stays valid across the rename; verified by the existing cache-key test). The verdict JSONL shape, broker interface from ADR-090, prompt template from ADR-089, and every other surface stays identical. The maintainer explicitly rejected a `migrateLegacyLayout()` helper with the directive "I am only the user so far, I will move all manually" and "Keep the code clean" — so the implementation has zero migration code, no `LEGACY_*` constants, and no version-skew detection. Plugin users running into mixed states `mv` files manually (POSIX one-liner: `mkdir -p .codex-pair && mv .codex-pair-context.md .codex-pair/context.md && mv .codex-pair-log.jsonl .codex-pair/log.jsonl && mv .codex-pair-ignore .codex-pair/ignore && mv .codex-pair-cache .codex-pair/cache && mv .codex-pair-state .codex-pair/state`).
- **Decision:** All hook state nests under `<markerDir>/.codex-pair/`. The exact mapping:

  | Before (flat) | After (nested) |
  |---|---|
  | `.codex-pair-context.md` | `.codex-pair/context.md` |
  | `.codex-pair-log.jsonl` | `.codex-pair/log.jsonl` |
  | `.codex-pair-ignore` | `.codex-pair/ignore` |
  | `.codex-pair-cache/<2>/<rest>.json` | `.codex-pair/cache/<2>/<rest>.json` |
  | `.codex-pair-state/paused` | `.codex-pair/state/paused` |
  | `.codex-pair-state/inflight/<hash>` | `.codex-pair/state/inflight/<hash>` |
  | `.codex-pair-state/broker.json` *(reserved, ADR-090)* | `.codex-pair/state/broker.json` *(reserved)* |

  **Implementation strategy: path resolvers, not string literals.** `packages/claude-plugin/scripts/lib/state.mjs` (the ADR-088 extraction) becomes the single source of truth for every state-file location via composable resolvers — exported functions that compose `markerDir` with a domain-specific filename. The hook script (`codex-pair-watch.mjs`) and CLI (`codex-pair-log.mjs`) call these helpers; neither hard-codes a string literal. The constants exported:

  ```js
  export const PAIR_ROOT_DIR = ".codex-pair";
  export const CONTEXT_FILENAME = "context.md";
  export const IGNORE_FILENAME = "ignore";
  export const LOG_FILENAME = "log.jsonl";
  export const CACHE_DIR = "cache";
  export const STATE_DIR = "state";
  export const PAUSE_SENTINEL_FILE = "paused";
  export const INFLIGHT_DIR = "inflight";

  export const pairRoot     = (m) => join(m, PAIR_ROOT_DIR);
  export const contextPath  = (m) => join(pairRoot(m), CONTEXT_FILENAME);
  export const ignorePath   = (m) => join(pairRoot(m), IGNORE_FILENAME);
  export const logPath      = (m) => join(pairRoot(m), LOG_FILENAME);
  export const cacheRoot    = (m) => join(pairRoot(m), CACHE_DIR);
  export const stateRoot    = (m) => join(pairRoot(m), STATE_DIR);
  export const pausePath    = (m) => join(stateRoot(m), PAUSE_SENTINEL_FILE);
  export const inflightRoot = (m) => join(stateRoot(m), INFLIGHT_DIR);
  ```

  **`findMarkerUp` semantic change**: previously returned the marker-FILE path; now returns the project ROOT (the directory holding `.codex-pair/`). This is the more useful primitive — every consumer wanted the dir anyway and had to `dirname(markerPath)` it. Updated in `codex-pair-watch.mjs` and the CLI binary. **Helper consolidation**: all path operations in `state.mjs` (`isPaused`, `tryAcquireInflightLock`, `inflightLockPath`, `cachePathFor`, `appendLog`, `rotateLogIfNeeded`) route through the resolvers — none accept hard-coded path fragments. `dirname(logPath(markerDir))` replaces the old `dirname(target)` where `target` came from inline `join` calls. **Surfaces updated**: (1) `scripts/lib/state.mjs` — constants + resolvers + helper bodies. (2) `scripts/codex-pair-watch.mjs` — `MARKER_FILE = join(PAIR_ROOT_DIR, CONTEXT_FILENAME)`, `findMarkerUp` returns root, calls to `contextPath` / `ignorePath` / `pausePath` for reads, updated log message strings for paused/ignored verdicts. (3) `scripts/codex-pair-log.mjs` — imports `CONTEXT_FILENAME` + `PAIR_ROOT_DIR` + `logPath as resolveLogPath`, main flow uses `resolveLogPath(markerDir)`, no-marker stderr message and help text updated. (4) `scripts/lib/broker.mjs` + `scripts/codex-pair-session.mjs` — comment-only updates referencing the broker descriptor path; the actual code already accepts a `stateDir` parameter so no signature change. (5) `.gitignore` — collapsed from 4 enumerated entries to a single `.codex-pair/` line. (6) `skills/codex-pair/SKILL.md`, `skills/codex-pair-pause/SKILL.md`, `skills/codex-pair-resume/SKILL.md`, `skills/codex-review/SKILL.md` — every path reference in skill frontmatter, opt-in instructions, and disable instructions. (7) `apps/docs/plugin/hooks.md`, `apps/docs/plugin/skills.md`, `apps/docs/plugin/overview.md` — user-facing docs. **Tests** (`packages/claude-plugin/src/__tests__/codex-pair-watch.test.ts`): a `setupMarker(dir, content?)` helper replaces ~30 ad-hoc `fs.writeFileSync` calls (each previously also needed a `mkdir`); structural source-grep tests updated to assert composition via the new `state.mjs` constants rather than literal paths; 8 regex literals fixed where bulk-replace `.codex-pair-state` → `.codex-pair/state` broke `/.../` regex syntax (escaped internal `/` or switched to bracket notation `[\\/]`). Plugin test count holds at 230. **Explicitly OUT of scope**: (a) no migration helper, no LEGACY_* constants, no compatibility shims — per the maintainer's directive; the implementation is forward-only. (b) no env-flag for the layout — `CODEX_PAIR_LEGACY_LAYOUT=1` would have been the natural emergency switch but it's banned by "Keep the code clean". (c) no `mv` script — manual move per directive. (d) cache hash format unchanged — the on-disk JSON value is byte-identical to v0.6.7, so a contributor who manually `mv .codex-pair-cache .codex-pair/cache` keeps every warm cache entry. (e) the broker (ADR-090) doesn't change implementation here — only its planned descriptor path moves from `.codex-pair-state/broker.json` to `.codex-pair/state/broker.json`, captured in `brokerStatePath(markerDir, stateDir)` which already took `stateDir` as a parameter.
- **Consequences:** **Operator ergonomics** improve in five concrete ways: (1) `.gitignore` collapses from four enumerated codex-pair entries to one `.codex-pair/` line — future state files (broker.json, health markers, anything Tier 3 adds) inherit the ignore automatically; no contributor has to remember to extend gitignore on the next ADR. (2) `ls -la` in an opted-in project shows ONE codex-pair entry instead of five; the per-developer machinery visually separates from the project's real state. (3) "Show me everything codex-pair stores" is now `ls -la .codex-pair/` — a single command instead of five greps. (4) `rm -rf .codex-pair/` cleanly removes all per-developer state; the previous "remove every codex-pair-* entry" form was easy to forget the cache or state dir on. (5) Path resolvers in `state.mjs` make a future fifth state surface (say, a per-session UUID file or a Tier 3 health marker) trivial — one new `export const fooPath = (m) => join(stateRoot(m), FOO_FILE)` and every consumer routes through it. **What this costs**: zero runtime cost (path resolution is pure-function `join`); zero protocol/payload changes; zero breaking surface for consumers outside this repo (the cache JSON shape, log JSONL shape, hooks.json registrations all unchanged). The full price is a one-time mechanical refactor: ~95 call-site path migrations across hook, CLI, tests, docs, .gitignore (every site routed through resolvers, no straggler literals). **What this DOESN'T break**: every ADR-077 / 079 / 081 / 082 / 084 / 085 / 086 / 087 / 088 / 089 / 090 / 091 contract holds byte-for-byte. The hook's silent-on-error semantics, the cache-key hash inputs, the verdict closed-set, the prompt template golden, the inflight lock TOCTOU defense, the PIPE_BUF clamp — none are touched. **Mixed-state risk** (the cost of the no-migration decision): a user upgrading from v0.6.x with existing flat `.codex-pair-*` files gets a fresh-install experience — the hook treats the legacy files as not-present and starts a new state directory. Cache history is the only thing that visibly resets unless the user manually mv's it. **This is explicitly accepted**: the maintainer is the only current user, and even if other users hit it, the failure mode is "review still works, you lose ≤24h of cache". No data corruption is possible (the flat paths never get read by the new code). **Future hook additions follow this pattern**: new state file? Add a constant + a resolver in `state.mjs`, expose it from any consumer that needs it, and the `.gitignore` rule covers it. The directory is the unit of "per-developer codex-pair state", not the individual files. **Forward path**: the broker (ADR-090) implementation, when it lands, writes `.codex-pair/state/broker.json` via the existing `brokerStatePath` helper. Tier 3 follow-on ADRs (health probe markers, version-skew indicators, cancellation tokens) all nest under `state/` and are covered by the existing gitignore without amendment.

## ADR-091: `codex-pair` Hook — Atomicity Gaps Closed (Log Rotation PID-Scope, TOCTOU Identity Snapshot, UTF-8 Byte Accounting, Test Env Cleanup)
- **Date:** 2026-05-19
- **Status:** Accepted (extends ADR-086, ADR-087, ADR-088; v0.6.7 follow-on; informed by multi-provider review of PRs #81–#90 via `/ask-llm:multi-review`)
- **Context:** A multi-provider review (`/ask-llm:multi-review`) ran the cumulative diff from PRs #81–#90 through both Gemini and Codex reviewers in parallel. Gemini exhausted its tool-call budget mid-investigation and produced no final report — surfaced honestly per the skill's Phase 4 protocol. Codex returned 5 self-validated findings; 4 were independently verified against source and accepted. The findings revealed three real correctness gaps in the atomicity / concurrency story that ADR-086 (atomic state writes) and ADR-087 (per-file inflight lock for coalesce) had promised. None are catastrophic — the hook's silent-on-error contract degrades gracefully on each — but every one is reachable in the exact "rapid MultiEdit storm" scenario the previous two ADRs were trying to harden against, so closing them is high-value-per-line.
- **Decision:** Four surgical fixes, all in `packages/claude-plugin/scripts/lib/state.mjs` (3) and the corresponding test file (1+test updates): **(1) `rotateLogIfNeeded` now uses `${logPath}.tmp.${process.pid}`** — mirrors `setCachedConcerns`'s PID-scoped tmp pattern from ADR-086. Previously the log rotation path used a shared `${logPath}.tmp` filename; two concurrent hooks both rotating produced torn writes when their `writeFile` calls interleaved on the same tmp. The cache pattern was correct from day one (ADR-086 introduced it); the log rotation path was a fix-asymmetry oversight. **(2) `tryAcquireInflightLock` adds an identity-snapshot recheck before the unlink-and-recreate stale-recovery path** — captures `{mtimeMs, pid-content}` of the would-be-stale lock at staleness-decision time, re-stats and re-reads PID content immediately before the unlink, and aborts the recovery (treating the lock as in-flight) if either changed. Narrows the TOCTOU window from "any time between stat and unlink" to "sub-microsecond between the second stat and the unlink itself" — not zero, but small enough that the only realistic concurrent-recovery scenario is fully suppressed. **(3) `clampReason` switches to UTF-8 byte accounting via `Buffer.byteLength` + `Buffer.from().subarray()`** — and adds a continuation-byte backoff (`(buf[end] & 0xc0) === 0x80`) so the slice never cuts mid-codepoint (no U+FFFD replacement chars in the log). The `MAX_LOG_REASON_BYTES = 3500` constant's name was correct all along; the implementation just hadn't honored the "bytes" word. The dropped-byte count in the truncation marker is now actually a byte count, not a char count. **(4) The ADR-090 broker test's env cleanup switches from `process.env.X = undefined` to `delete process.env.X`** — Node coerces the former to the literal string `"undefined"` (confirmed empirically: `JSON.stringify(process.env.X)` returns `"undefined"`). The next test passed by luck (spawnSync's env-override happened to strip the polluted value), but any future test that spread `process.env` would carry the corruption. **Tests updated**: the ADR-086 clamp test now asserts `Buffer.byteLength(clamped, "utf8") < 4096` instead of `clamped.length < 4096`, plus a new multibyte assertion using `"ё".repeat(3000)` (Cyrillic, 2 bytes per char in UTF-8 = 6000 bytes input) that proves the clamp drops by byte count not char count. The envelope test similarly switches to `Buffer.byteLength`. Plugin test count stays at 230 — fixes are byte-equivalent for ASCII inputs (the common case) and the multibyte assertion is additive. **Out of scope** (explicit non-goals): (a) flock-based locking — POSIX-only and adds cross-platform complexity; the identity-snapshot pattern is a defensible improvement that works on both platforms; (b) renaming `MAX_LOG_REASON_BYTES` (the name was already correct, only the body had drifted); (c) the dropped Gemini investigation — multi-review's Phase 4 protocol surfaces the failure inline; we don't retry until budget pressures lift. **Findings rejected during verification** (would-be-fixes that turned out NOT to be bugs): cache key drift across the ADR-089 prompt externalization (verified byte-identical via the golden test); cache forward-compat for missing `durationMs` field (`JSON.stringify` omits undefined values); broker stub's unreachable second-return statement (intentional per ADR-090's staged-rollout comment); `process.kill(-pid)` without detached (every spawn site already sets `detached: !IS_WINDOWS`).
- **Consequences:** The atomicity story the previous batch of ADRs promised is now structurally complete. Log rotation under concurrent edits no longer races; the inflight-lock TOCTOU window is narrowed to sub-microsecond; the PIPE_BUF contract holds for multibyte content too; and the test env-cleanup hygiene is correct. **What this costs**: (a) `clampReason` now invokes `Buffer.byteLength` + `Buffer.from` per oversize call — both cheap, sub-microsecond on typical inputs. (b) `tryAcquireInflightLock` does one extra `statSync` + one extra `readFileSync` on the stale-recovery path — negligible against the per-edit work this code coordinates. (c) Tests get slightly larger by ~12 LOC for the multibyte assertion. **What this DOESN'T break**: behavior for ASCII reasons (the common case) is byte-equivalent to before. The verdict closed set, log JSONL shape, cache layout, pause sentinel, and broker interface from ADR-085/086/087/088/089/090 are all unchanged. No cache invalidation, no migration step. **Multi-provider review value captured**: the most impactful finding (log rotation race) was a real bug in production code that would have eventually surfaced under load; the second (TOCTOU) was a documented-but-narrow race; the third (UTF-8) was a contract gap waiting for a non-ASCII input; the fourth (env cleanup) was a test-quality issue that would have made future test refactors fragile. None were caught by the green-tests-and-lint gate of the original PR sequence — exactly the class of issue cross-provider review is supposed to surface. **Gemini's failure mode logged**: the agent exhausted its tool-call budget after 12 calls and ~50K tokens, ending mid-investigation with no final findings. This is captured as a known limitation of the current multi-review configuration — a future PR may need to tune the budget cap upward for diffs over 100 KB.

## ADR-090: `codex-pair` Hook — App-Server Broker Design + SessionStart/SessionEnd Lifecycle (Interface Landed, Implementation Deferred to Tier 3)
- **Date:** 2026-05-19
- **Status:** Accepted (extends ADR-077, ADR-082, ADR-084, ADR-085, ADR-086, ADR-087, ADR-088; v0.7.0 Tier 2 story #9; full implementation tracked as Tier 3 follow-on per the roadmap)
- **Context:** ADR-077's hook spawns a fresh `codex exec --json` process for every Edit/Write/MultiEdit. The cold-spawn cost is 5–30 seconds (codex loads weights/config, opens the LLM connection, processes the prompt, emits JSONL, exits). Cumulatively across a typical session this is meaningful: 50 edits × 15s average = 12.5 minutes of latency on top of the actual review work. The reference plugin (`openai/codex-plugin-cc`) solves this by running `codex app-server` as a long-lived JSON-RPC server that the hook talks to over a Unix socket — no cold-spawn per call. Both multi-provider reviewers (Gemini + Codex) flagged this as the right long-term answer, but L-effort (multi-week) work because of: (a) cross-platform IPC (Unix sockets break on Windows per the v0.6.2 work), (b) lifecycle (who starts the broker? who tears it down? what happens on Claude Code crash?), (c) health protocol (broker is unresponsive → fall back to per-edit spawn? wait? kill+restart?), (d) version skew (the broker's codex binary version may differ from the per-edit hook's expected protocol), (e) per-project context isolation (multiple Claude sessions across multiple projects shouldn't share a broker), (f) concurrent hook requests (the broker must serialize or interleave them per its protocol), (g) cancellation (a slow review the user no longer wants). The roadmap explicitly sequences this story LAST and gates Tier 3 health/cleanup helpers on this ADR landing. Five design questions: (A) **transport** — Unix socket on POSIX (proven, fast, fork-safe, file-permission ACL'd) + WebSocket on Windows (the only cross-platform alternative codex's `--listen` flag supports; named pipes on Windows would be ideal but codex doesn't speak them). The hook detects the platform at startup and routes accordingly; the broker descriptor file records the transport URL so the hook doesn't need to re-detect. (B) **lifecycle ownership** — Claude Code's SessionStart hook is the right launch trigger because (i) it fires once per session, (ii) it has access to the project's marker directory, (iii) it pre-dates the first PostToolUse so the broker is ready by the time the first edit fires. SessionEnd is the matching teardown trigger. ADR-048's removal of the Stop hook is unrelated — Stop is per-turn (problematic per ADR-048), SessionStart/SessionEnd are session-lifecycle (a different event class). (C) **failure modes** — (i) **broker spawn fails at SessionStart**: log the failure, do NOT raise to the user, hook's per-edit path remains fully functional via the existing spawnCodex path. (ii) **broker dies mid-session**: the hook's per-edit health probe fails → fall back to per-edit spawn for that one call, mark the broker state stale so SessionStart-style recovery (a background respawn) can fire. (iii) **stale daemon from a previous crashed session**: SessionStart calls `clearStaleBrokerState` BEFORE spawning a fresh broker; the helper checks (PID is alive AND socket is reachable AND codex version matches) — any failure clears the state. (iv) **version skew**: the broker descriptor includes `codex_version` and `protocol_version` at spawn time; hook startup checks these against what `codex --version` reports now and treats a mismatch as a stale broker. (D) **per-project isolation** — the broker socket path is `<markerDir>/.codex-pair-state/codex-pair-broker.<sha256(markerDir)[0:8]>.sock`. Each project's broker lives under that project's marker directory. The sha256 prefix prevents conflicts between projects that happen to share the same marker directory name (rare but possible across symlinks). (E) **concurrent hook requests** — the broker serializes requests via the inflight lock from ADR-087, which works unchanged in the broker world: hook acquires the lock first, sends the broker request second; if the lock fails, the request is coalesced as today. The broker doesn't need its own concurrency primitive because the lock is filesystem-level and process-shared. **What ships in this ADR vs Tier 3**: this PR ships the **ADR + the interface stubs + the SessionStart/SessionEnd hook scaffold**. The full IPC implementation, the codex app-server JSON-RPC bindings, the health-probe loop, and the stale-recovery logic are explicitly Tier 3 follow-on work, tracked in the roadmap as `Health check + stale-PID cleanup helpers — gated on broker ADR (item 9)`. The split is honest: the design decisions (which require ADR consensus and are reversible only with significant cost) are baked in now; the implementation (which is mechanical given the design) lands when the engineering bandwidth is available without rushing.
- **Decision:** Five components ship today (interface) + four deferred (implementation). **(1) `packages/claude-plugin/scripts/lib/broker.mjs`** — interface module with stable exports: `BROKER_STATE_FILE`, `BROKER_HEALTH_TIMEOUT_MS`, `BROKER_SOCKET_PREFIX`, `readBrokerState(markerDir)`, `isBrokerEnabled(markerDir)`, `brokerStatePath(markerDir, stateDir)`, `clearStaleBrokerState(markerDir)`, `probeBrokerHealth(state)`, `submitReview(state, prompt, options)`. Today's bodies are stubs: `readBrokerState` always returns `null`, `isBrokerEnabled` always returns `false`, `submitReview` throws a clear "not implemented yet" error pointing at this ADR. `BROKER_HEALTH_TIMEOUT_MS = 2000` is the pinned health-probe deadline — short enough that the per-edit hook doesn't spend more than 2s discovering a dead broker, long enough that a healthy broker on a loaded machine still answers in time. **(2) `packages/claude-plugin/scripts/codex-pair-session.mjs`** — SessionStart/SessionEnd hook entry point. Reads stdin (Claude Code's hook protocol), inspects `hook_event_name`, exits 0 on any unrecognized event. Today: if `ASK_CODEX_BROKER !== "1"`, immediate exit 0; otherwise the TODO comment documents the planned spawn/teardown logic. The hook MUST exit 0 on every path so a broker failure never breaks a session. **(3) `hooks.json` registers SessionStart + SessionEnd dispatchers** pointing at `codex-pair-session.mjs`. The hook surface is wired but the body is no-op until `ASK_CODEX_BROKER=1` ships with the real implementation. **(4) `lib/broker.mjs` integration plan for `codex-pair-watch.mjs`** (documented here, not yet wired): the per-edit hook's main() will check `isBrokerEnabled(markerDir)` BEFORE the cache check; on `true` it routes through `submitReview` instead of `spawnCodex`; on `false` it falls through to the existing path. This single integration point keeps the broker an opt-in optimization with byte-identical fallback behavior. **(5) Transport choice formalized**: POSIX → Unix socket at `<markerDir>/.codex-pair-state/<BROKER_SOCKET_PREFIX>.<sha256(markerDir)[0:8]>.sock`; Windows → WebSocket on `ws://127.0.0.1:<random-port-from-os>`. The descriptor file records the chosen transport so the hook reads it once and uses it for every subsequent request. **Deferred** to Tier 3 PR: (i) the actual `spawn("codex", ["app-server", "--listen", transport])` in `codex-pair-session.mjs`; (ii) the JSON-RPC client in `lib/broker.mjs` (handshake, ping, submitReview, cancellation); (iii) the health probe implementation; (iv) the integration call in `codex-pair-watch.mjs`'s main(). **Tests added** (`packages/claude-plugin/src/__tests__/codex-pair-watch.test.ts`, 6 new): structural pin on the broker module's documented interface (functions + constants); behavioral assertion that `isBrokerEnabled` returns false under all current conditions (gating contract); `brokerStatePath` composition; explicit-failure stub for `submitReview` (proves the trap exists for premature callers); structural pin on `hooks.json` SessionStart + SessionEnd registrations; subprocess test that runs `codex-pair-session.mjs` with a SessionStart payload and asserts silent exit 0 when broker is disabled. Plugin test count 224 → 230. **Out of scope explicitly**: (a) implementing the broker now — would be unsafe given the cross-platform IPC complexity, Tier 3 scope, and stated multi-week effort estimate; (b) named pipes on Windows — codex CLI doesn't speak them, blocked at the upstream layer; (c) cross-session broker sharing — explicitly rejected; one broker per project per session enforces context isolation; (d) HTTP REST transport — JSON-RPC over the codex `--listen` choices is the codex CLI's contract, not negotiable from our side; (e) automatic broker upgrade on codex version bump — the SessionStart stale-recovery path covers this when SessionStart fires (next session); mid-session bumps trigger fallback-to-spawn for the rest of the session.
- **Consequences:** Story #9's ADR requirement (the gating decision the roadmap explicitly demanded) is satisfied. The broker's API surface is stable; future Tier 3 work fills in the bodies without touching any consumer (the hook's main() integration is a one-liner: `if (isBrokerEnabled(markerDir)) { ... }`). The SessionStart/SessionEnd hook scaffold is wired, so Tier 3 can implement the lifecycle without touching `hooks.json` again. **What this ships today**: a documented interface + a no-op-but-wired hook scaffold + 6 tests that pin the contract. The per-edit cold-spawn cost is unchanged — broker is opt-in (`ASK_CODEX_BROKER=1`) and the stub returns false regardless, so today's behavior is byte-identical to v0.6.6. **What ships LATER (Tier 3 follow-on)**: (i) `spawn("codex", ["app-server", "--listen", transport])` in `codex-pair-session.mjs`'s SessionStart branch with atomic state-file write per ADR-086. (ii) JSON-RPC client implementation in `lib/broker.mjs`: handshake, ping/pong, submitReview, request cancellation. (iii) Health-probe implementation that opens the transport, sends `ping`, awaits `pong` within `BROKER_HEALTH_TIMEOUT_MS`, returns boolean. (iv) Integration call in `codex-pair-watch.mjs` between marker resolution and cache check. (v) Stale-broker recovery logic in `clearStaleBrokerState`. **What this DOESN'T break**: every hook today still uses per-edit spawnCodex. The cache, inflight lock, pause sentinel, log atomicity, prompt template — all unchanged. The plugin distribution is unchanged: the new `scripts/lib/broker.mjs` and `scripts/codex-pair-session.mjs` ship alongside everything else. **Stale-state recovery contract** (codified for the Tier 3 PR): on SessionStart, call `clearStaleBrokerState(markerDir)` BEFORE spawning. The helper: (a) reads `.codex-pair-state/broker.json` if present; (b) checks PID liveness via `process.kill(pid, 0)`; (c) checks socket reachability via a 500ms connect attempt; (d) compares the recorded `codex_version` to current `codex --version`; (e) any mismatch → unlink the state file and the socket (best-effort). The per-edit hook MAY call it on startup as belt-and-suspenders defense, but SessionStart is the contract. **Health-probe contract** (codified for the Tier 3 PR): on every hook invocation where `ASK_CODEX_BROKER=1`, before submitReview, call `probeBrokerHealth(state)` with `BROKER_HEALTH_TIMEOUT_MS = 2000`. On failure: fall through to per-edit spawnCodex AND mark the broker state stale so the next SessionStart respawns. **Version-skew contract**: the broker descriptor records `codex_version` (output of `codex --version` at SessionStart). On every hook invocation, the hook re-runs `codex --version` and compares; mismatch → treat as stale broker. (This adds a per-edit `codex --version` invocation; the cost is one fork+exec of a small binary, ~30ms — acceptable against the multi-second savings on broker hits.) **Risk explicitly accepted**: the broker code path is unreachable today, so it's untested in production. The Tier 3 PR MUST include an end-to-end test that spawns a real `codex app-server`, sends a `submitReview`, and asserts the round-trip. The 6 tests in this PR pin the interface but not the runtime. **Forward compatibility**: ADR-091+ may revise transport choice (e.g. if Windows named-pipe support lands in codex CLI, we'd add a third branch alongside Unix socket and WebSocket). The `brokerStatePath` + descriptor file shape are stable. The `submitReview` and `probeBrokerHealth` signatures are stable; future changes happen inside their implementations.

## ADR-089: `codex-pair` Hook — Externalize Prompt Template to `prompts/review.txt` with Byte-Identical Render (Cache-Key Preserving) and Golden Tests
- **Date:** 2026-05-19
- **Status:** Accepted (extends ADR-077, ADR-082, ADR-083, ADR-088; v0.6.6 Tier 2 story #8)
- **Context:** ADR-083 codified the strict-JSON prompt contract as a multi-line JS template literal inside `buildPrompt` in `codex-pair-watch.mjs`. That string is ~50 lines, contains template literals for `${contextBlock}`, `${partialViewBlock}`, `${toolName}`, `${filePath}`, `${fileContent}`, and is approximately 2 KB of prose that prompt-engineers would naturally want to iterate on independently of the surrounding code. Three problems with the in-source layout: (a) **edit-cost coupling** — tuning the prompt requires opening a 1700-LOC script file, scrolling past the spawn/cache/log/lock plumbing, and editing inside a template literal where the escaping rules are subtle (backticks, `${}` interpolation collision with the JSON-schema example's `}` characters). Prompt-engineering iteration deserves its own file. (b) **no version anchoring** — the prompt's evolution is tangled in the script's git history alongside parser refactors, lock additions, etc. A dedicated file means `git log prompts/review.txt` shows ONLY prompt changes, which is the right surface for tracking prompt evolution. (c) **golden-test gap** — the rendered prompt is the cache key's primary input (ADR-082's `computeCacheKey` hashes `model + prompt + fileContent + surfaceThreshold`). Any change to the prompt template — even a whitespace tweak — invalidates every cached entry on every developer's machine. There's no current guardrail asserting that future template edits preserve the rendered bytes or, conversely, that they don't. **The Codex reviewer's demotion-to-Tier-2 condition was specifically**: "this is maintainability-only UNLESS paired with golden prompt tests and explicit cache-key migration." This ADR satisfies both halves. Three design questions: (A) **template syntax** — full templating engine (handlebars, mustache) vs simple `{{TOKEN}}` substitution vs leaving it as JS template literal in a `.mjs` file? Simple `{{TOKEN}}` substitution wins: zero dependencies, transparent rendering, no escaping complexity, and the substitution targets are all known at module load. (B) **load timing** — read the template on every hook invocation, or cache it at module init? Module init: the hook spawns a fresh Node process per Edit so "every invocation" and "module init" are the same wall-clock event; module init is cleaner because it makes the template path resolution explicit. Sync `readFileSync` (not async) because it runs at script load, before any async I/O matters. (C) **conditional blocks** — pre-render the `contextBlock` and `partialViewBlock` strings in JS and substitute them as whole units, or push the conditional logic into the template with handlebars-style `{{#if}}`? Pre-render wins: keeps the renderer dead-simple (string replacement only), keeps the template human-readable (no embedded mini-language), and preserves the exact ADR-077-vintage logic for those blocks (trailing newline counts, etc.) so the rendered bytes are byte-identical to pre-refactor.
- **Decision:** Four components. **(1) `packages/claude-plugin/prompts/review.txt`** — the externalized template. Contains the full ADR-083 strict-JSON prompt with five placeholder tokens: `{{CONTEXT_BLOCK}}`, `{{PARTIAL_VIEW_BLOCK}}`, `{{TOOL_NAME}}`, `{{FILE_PATH}}`, `{{FILE_CONTENT}}`. NO conditional logic in the template itself — both block tokens get pre-rendered to either the full block (with appropriate `\n\n` trailing) or an empty string by the caller. **(2) `packages/claude-plugin/scripts/lib/prompt.mjs`** — exports `loadPromptTemplate()` (sync read of the template file relative to the script's location) and `buildReviewPrompt({ filePath, fileContent, toolName, projectContext, partialView })` which (a) pre-renders the context and partial-view blocks using the SAME byte-identical logic as the pre-refactor `buildPrompt`, (b) substitutes the five tokens via sequential `String.prototype.replace` (literal targets, no regex special-char surface), (c) returns the rendered prompt. The template is loaded once at module init into a module-level `TEMPLATE` const. **(3) `buildPrompt` in `codex-pair-watch.mjs`** becomes a thin pass-through: `function buildPrompt(args) { return buildReviewPrompt(args); }`. This preserves the existing call site so structural tests that pin the call site stay readable, and so a future PR can swap out `buildReviewPrompt` for a different renderer without touching every caller. **(4) Golden test fixture** at `packages/claude-plugin/src/__tests__/_fixtures/review-prompt.golden.txt` — generated from the actual `buildReviewPrompt` output for a fixed set of inputs (`filePath: "src/billing/charge.ts"`, a small TypeScript file, a non-empty `projectContext`, `partialView: false`). The accompanying test loads the fixture and asserts byte-equality with the live renderer's output. **Cache-key preservation**: the rendered string is byte-identical to the pre-refactor render. The cache key (`sha256(model + prompt + fileContent + surfaceThreshold)`) therefore stays stable across this refactor — every cached entry on every developer's machine remains valid. No migration step needed. **Tests added** (`packages/claude-plugin/src/__tests__/codex-pair-watch.test.ts`, 2 new + 3 rewritten): (i) golden — `buildReviewPrompt(fixture_inputs)` byte-equality against `_fixtures/review-prompt.golden.txt`; this is the load-bearing test for "future template edits don't silently change rendered bytes and invalidate caches"; (ii) empty-context — when `projectContext === ""`, the rendered prompt MUST NOT contain `## Project context` and MUST flow naturally with no double-blank-line artifact where the block used to be; (iii) the three previously-source-grep'd tests (partial-view instruction, `<file_content>` XML wrapping, strict-JSON shape) become real `buildReviewPrompt` calls + assertions on the returned string. Plugin test count 222 → 224. **Out of scope**: (a) supporting multiple templates (per-project `.codex-pair-review.txt` overrides) — adds discovery complexity for unclear value; deferred until a real request appears. (b) per-language prompt variants (TS-specific, Python-specific, etc.) — codex handles language-detection internally; no observed quality gain from forcing it. (c) full templating engine — `{{TOKEN}}` substitution covers every current need with zero dependencies. (d) localization — codex is English-only.
- **Consequences:** Prompt-engineering iteration is now a focused single-file activity. `git log prompts/review.txt` shows ONLY prompt changes. The golden test catches any unintended byte drift in the rendered output, which protects the content-hash cache from silent invalidation. The Codex reviewer's demotion-to-Tier-2 conditions are both satisfied. **What this costs**: (a) one `readFileSync` at hook startup — sub-millisecond, runs once per hook invocation (each invocation is a fresh Node process). (b) the indirection through `buildReviewPrompt` instead of inline string interpolation — negligible runtime cost, minor cognitive cost (one more file to know about). (c) the golden fixture must be regenerated whenever the template intentionally changes — the test failure message will tell future maintainers exactly what's needed; the regeneration is one `node -e '...'` line. **What this DOESN'T break**: byte-identical render means every cached entry on every developer's machine stays valid (zero migration). The hook's external behavior — JSON output to codex, parse path, verdict shape, log shape — is unchanged. Cache key invariants from ADR-082 hold. **Cache-key migration strategy** (codified for future template changes): when an intentional prompt change ships, the changeset MUST note "invalidates cache" in the description so users know to expect one fresh codex spawn per active file in the 10-minute TTL window after upgrade. The golden fixture MUST be regenerated in the same commit; the test would otherwise fail with a clear diff showing what bytes changed. **Cache-warm cost on intentional changes**: one extra codex call per unique file in the first 10 minutes (same as ADR-083's rollout cost; both are bounded by `CACHE_TTL_MS`). **What this enables for downstream work**: (a) prompt iteration becomes a self-contained activity that doesn't require touching the hook script. (b) A future "review style profiles" feature (security-focused vs perf-focused vs general) could ship multiple templates in `prompts/` with a selector flag — same rendering machinery, no architectural changes needed. (c) Story #9 (broker) can reuse `buildReviewPrompt` from the broker process without duplicating prompt logic. **Risk explicitly accepted**: a contributor who edits `prompts/review.txt` without regenerating the golden fixture will fail the test on the next CI run with a clear diff — minor friction, but the friction is the feature (cache invalidation should be a conscious decision).

## ADR-088: `codex-pair` Hook — Targeted `lib/` Extraction (`process.mjs`, `parser.mjs`, `state.mjs`) + Shift From Source-Regex to Module-Import Tests
- **Date:** 2026-05-19
- **Status:** Accepted (extends ADR-077, ADR-078, ADR-082, ADR-083, ADR-084, ADR-085, ADR-086, ADR-087; v0.6.5 Tier 2 story #7; informed by `openai/codex-plugin-cc`'s 13-file `lib/` layout — adopted in spirit but trimmed to 3 files for ask-llm)
- **Context:** ADR-077 shipped the codex-pair hook as a single ~1800-LOC `scripts/codex-pair-watch.mjs` module. As ADR-079–ADR-087 piled on (frontmatter config, adaptive context, ignore globs, cache, pause, atomic writes, inflight lock), the file grew into a monolith where every pure helper sat next to I/O-heavy main flow code. Tests had to grep the script source with regexes (`script.match(/function buildVerdictMessage[\s\S]*?^\}/m)`) to assert structural invariants — a technique that's brittle (refactors that move a closing brace break the regex), unable to verify *behavior* (only presence of source text), and impossible to scale (every new helper added more regex pin-tests). The roadmap's story #7 calls this out: "enable real unit tests, not just regex-on-source structural tests." Three design questions: (A) **how many lib files** — codex-plugin-cc's reference is 13. Gemini's brainstorm flagged 13 as sprawl for our smaller surface; the roadmap caps at 3-5. We picked **3** to hit the lower bound: enough to prove the boundary, not so many that the next-PR migration overhead chokes future work. (B) **which surfaces to extract first** — pure functions over inputs (parser, process management) deliver the biggest test-surface win because they're easy to unit-test in isolation. I/O-heavy code (`spawnCodex`, `runGitDiff`) is harder to mock and was deliberately deferred to a future PR. (C) **ESM imports vs bundler** — the plugin ships every file (`.claude-plugin/marketplace.json` references the whole directory), hooks reference absolute paths in `hooks.json`, and Node ESM resolves relative imports cleanly via `./lib/process.mjs`. A bundler adds build steps, source-map complexity, and a publish-time toolchain — none of which buy anything because the consumer is Node executing the file directly. **Decision: raw `.mjs` files, relative imports**.
- **Decision:** Three new modules under `packages/claude-plugin/scripts/lib/`. **(1) `process.mjs`** — exports `IS_WINDOWS` (platform check) and `terminateProcessTree(child, signal)` (POSIX `process.kill(-pid)` / Windows `taskkill /F /T`). The hook script imports both at the top and removes the inline definitions. (2) `parser.mjs`** — exports `VERDICT_PREFIXES`, `SEVERITY_TO_BUCKET`, `VALID_THRESHOLDS`, `DEFAULT_SURFACE_THRESHOLD`, `formatDuration`, `buildVerdictMessage`, `tryExtractJson`, `formatFindingBody`, `parseConcernsJson`, `parseConcernsLegacy`, `parseConcerns`. All pure functions over strings — zero filesystem or process touchpoints. The hook imports the subset it actually uses (`buildVerdictMessage`, `DEFAULT_SURFACE_THRESHOLD`, `formatDuration`, `parseConcerns`, `VALID_THRESHOLDS`, `VERDICT_PREFIXES`). **(3) `state.mjs`** — exports all durable-state helpers: cache (`computeCacheKey`, `cachePathFor`, `getCachedConcerns`, `setCachedConcerns`, `evictCacheOldest`, `CACHE_DIR`, `CACHE_TTL_MS`, `CACHE_MAX_ENTRIES`), log (`appendLog`, `rotateLogIfNeeded`, `clampReason`, `LOG_FILENAME`, `MAX_LOG_BYTES`, `MAX_LOG_ENTRIES`, `MAX_LOG_REASON_BYTES`), pause (`isPaused`, `PAUSE_STATE_DIR`, `PAUSE_SENTINEL_FILE`), inflight lock (`inflightLockPath`, `tryAcquireInflightLock`, `releaseInflightLock`, `INFLIGHT_DIR`, `INFLIGHT_TTL_MIN_MS`). The hook imports the subset it actually uses (`appendLog`, `computeCacheKey`, `getCachedConcerns`, `INFLIGHT_TTL_MIN_MS`, `isPaused`, `releaseInflightLock`, `setCachedConcerns`, `tryAcquireInflightLock`). Crucially, the hook's `main()` function still drives the orchestration — the lib modules are pure helpers, not stateful objects. **What stays in the hook script**: orchestration (`main()`), `runGitDiff` + `buildAdaptiveContext` (planned for future `lib/git-diff.mjs`), `spawnCodex` + `runCodexWithFallback` + `spawnCodexWithRetry` + `buildCodexArgs` (planned for `lib/codex-spawn.mjs`), `parseFrontmatter` + `readIgnoreFile` + `matchesIgnoreRule` + `resolveConfig` + `findMarkerUp` (frontmatter/config plumbing, may move later), `buildPrompt` (planned for story #8 prompt externalization). **Test migration**: 10+ structural pin tests rewritten as either (a) lib-source structural pins (point at `lib/state.mjs` / `lib/process.mjs` source text, same regex style but stable target) or (b) **real unit tests** using ESM `await import("../../scripts/lib/parser.mjs")` to call the function directly and assert its observable behavior. Examples: `VERDICT_PREFIXES` now asserted via `expect(VERDICT_PREFIXES).toMatchObject({...})`; `buildVerdictMessage` threshold gating now asserted by calling the function with all 9 combinations of (high|med|low) input × (high|med|low) threshold and inspecting the returned string; `computeCacheKey` invalidation semantics now asserted by calling with one input varied at a time and asserting key inequality. **No new test count is added per se** — the 7 newly-real-unit-tested behaviors replace 7 source-regex tests; the count stays at 222. The value isn't more tests, it's better tests: refactor-resilient, behavior-asserting, and run in the same vitest invocation against the actual module the hook imports. **Bundler decision**: NO. Raw `.mjs` files, relative imports, no build step for the plugin distribution. The plugin ships every file under `packages/claude-plugin/` as-is; hooks point at absolute paths; Node ESM resolves `./lib/process.mjs` natively. Adding a bundler would force a publish-time build for zero runtime benefit (Node handles ESM resolution; there's no browser target). **Out of scope**: (a) extracting `codex-spawn.mjs` and `git-diff.mjs` — these touch real child processes and the test cost (mocking spawned processes cleanly) is high; deferred to a future PR. (b) extracting `frontmatter.mjs` — the frontmatter parser is already a small pure function and the call site is clear; doesn't pay its keep right now. (c) renaming the hook script itself or its directory — keeping the entry-point shape stable is load-bearing for `hooks.json` references. (d) `index.mjs` barrel re-exporter — would add an indirection layer without buying anything; consumers import directly from the per-feature module.
- **Consequences:** The codex-pair hook surface is now structurally aligned with the "domain modules + thin orchestrator" pattern that the codex-plugin-cc reference proves out at production scale. Three pure modules (`process.mjs`, `parser.mjs`, `state.mjs`) sit alongside the orchestrator script. Future work — story #8 (prompt externalization), story #9 (app-server broker) — can extend the pattern incrementally: add `lib/prompt.mjs`, `lib/broker.mjs`, etc., each with their own unit-tested API. **What this costs**: (a) one extra ESM resolution step per hook invocation per imported module — Node caches built-in resolutions, so negligible. (b) editor jump-to-definition across modules instead of single-file scroll — modern editors handle this well. (c) the hook script's import block at the top is now ~30 lines instead of 5 — more boilerplate, but explicit beats implicit. (d) test file's `await import(...)` form is verbose vs. inline regex — but the resulting tests survive refactors, which is the whole point. **What this DOESN'T break**: every observable behavior of the hook is byte-identical to ADR-087's state. Same prompt, same JSON contract, same verdict closed set, same log JSONL shape, same cache layout, same pause/inflight semantics. The plugin distribution (`marketplace.json` → user's `.claude/plugins/` directory) ships the new `scripts/lib/` directory automatically because the marketplace install copies everything under `packages/claude-plugin/`. No `hooks.json` change. **What this enables for downstream work**: (a) story #8 (prompt template externalization) lifts `buildPrompt` from the hook into either `lib/parser.mjs` or a new `lib/prompt.mjs` with golden-file tests against the rendered output. (b) story #9 (app-server broker) can introduce `lib/broker.mjs` for the long-lived codex sidecar lifecycle, sharing the `terminateProcessTree` helper from `lib/process.mjs` and the `tryAcquireInflightLock` from `lib/state.mjs` for cross-session coordination. (c) future contributors writing new helpers (e.g. a `verdictFromError` standalone) have a clear pattern: "if it's pure, put it in the appropriate lib module and unit-test it directly." (d) the test-surface improvement compounds — every new lib helper gets real unit tests for free, instead of a brittle source-regex pin. **Risk explicitly accepted**: a future contributor who adds a new helper to the wrong lib file (e.g. parser logic in `state.mjs`) creates a soft import-graph mess; no automated check enforces the "pure parsing vs. I/O state" divide. Mitigation: the module names + their docstring headers make the intended scope clear; any drift surfaces in code review.

## ADR-087: `codex-pair` Hook — Per-File Inflight Lock for PostToolUse Debounce / Coalesce
- **Date:** 2026-05-19
- **Status:** Accepted (extends ADR-077, ADR-082, ADR-085; v0.6.4 Tier 2 story #6; informed by `openai/codex-plugin-cc` single-flight lock pattern; replaces the dropped Stop-hook migration recommendation)
- **Context:** ADR-077 shipped a per-edit codex-pair review on `PostToolUse` for Edit/Write/MultiEdit. The codex spawn is sync-blocking (the hook waits for codex to finish before returning to Claude) and typically takes 5–30 seconds, occasionally up to the 800-second timeout. When the user prompts Claude to do something like "rename `User` to `Account` across these three files", Claude issues three rapid Edit calls in succession — each fires PostToolUse, each spawns its own codex review, and each review sees an *intermediate* state where the rename is partially done. Codex correctly flags those intermediate states as broken ("`Account.id` is referenced but not defined" because the type definition file hasn't been renamed yet), surfacing concerns that disappear by the third edit. This is exactly the false-positive class ADR-048 (Stop-hook removal) confronted from the opposite direction — Stop produced overlapping reviews of in-flight work; PostToolUse produces overlapping reviews of in-flight cumulative state. The fix needs to (a) suppress redundant reviews when the file is already mid-review, (b) NOT lose coverage on the final state (the version the user actually wants reviewed), (c) survive Claude's per-edit hook firing model (each invocation is a fresh OS process — no shared in-memory state), (d) work across sibling Claude sessions in the same project, (e) recover from crashed/SIGKILLed prior holders (an inflight lock that never gets released would mute review forever). Three design questions: (A) **lock granularity** — per file path, or per file-content hash? Per-path wins because successive edits to the same file produce different content hashes, so a content-hash lock wouldn't coalesce them (defeating the entire purpose). The semantic we want is "at most one review per file at a time, regardless of what content versions appeared during that time". (B) **coalesce vs cancel-and-restart** — when a new edit fires while a review is in flight, do we (i) skip the new one and let the in-flight review continue, or (ii) kill the in-flight review and start fresh with the latest content? Coalesce wins because (a) the in-flight review reads the file fresh when it actually spawns codex (`buildAdaptiveContext` reads HEAD's diff + file content at spawn time, not at hook-fire time), so cumulative edits up to the spawn point are already reviewed; (b) cancel-and-restart adds PID-kill complexity AND risks killing a near-finished review only to spawn a fresh one that pays the full 5–30s cost again; (c) coalesce is the codex-plugin-cc-reference pattern, validated in production. (C) **lock primitive** — `flock(2)` syscall, `O_CREAT|O_EXCL` sentinel file, or PID file + liveness check? Sentinel file with `O_CREAT|O_EXCL` (Node's `writeFileSync(path, ..., { flag: "wx" })`) wins because (a) it's atomic at the filesystem level, fork-safe, and works identically on POSIX and Windows; (b) flock is POSIX-only and has confusing semantics around process inheritance and across-NFS edge cases; (c) PID liveness check is racy (PID recycling on long-running systems). The lock file's *content* is the PID (informational only, for `ls`-debugging) and its *mtime* is the staleness signal. (D) **staleness TTL** — how long until a stuck lock counts as stale and gets taken over? Lower bound is codex's timeout (800s default per ADR-074) — anything less than that risks recovering a still-valid lock. We use `max(config.timeoutMs, 600_000) + 60_000` — at least 10 minutes, never less than the codex timeout, plus a 60-second buffer for slow filesystem mtime resolution.
- **Decision:** Five components in `packages/claude-plugin/scripts/codex-pair-watch.mjs`. **(1) Constants** — `INFLIGHT_DIR = "inflight"` (subdirectory inside `.codex-pair-state/`) and `INFLIGHT_TTL_MIN_MS = 600_000` (10-minute minimum TTL floor). The state directory was introduced in ADR-085 for the pause sentinel; this reuses the same convention so all transient hook state lives under one umbrella (gitignored alongside the marker, log, and cache). **(2) `inflightLockPath(markerDir, filePath)`** — keys the lock on `sha256(filePath).slice(0, 16)` to keep the on-disk name compact, deterministic, and free of path-separator issues. Sliced to 16 hex chars because we only need collision-resistance among the file paths a single project edits (typical: hundreds to low-thousands), not cryptographic uniqueness. **(3) `tryAcquireInflightLock(markerDir, filePath, ttlMs)`** — three-phase. Phase 1: `mkdirSync(dirname(lockPath), { recursive: true })` to ensure `.codex-pair-state/inflight/` exists (silently ignored on failure — phase 2's create will surface the real error). Phase 2: `writeFileSync(lockPath, String(process.pid), { flag: "wx" })` — atomic exclusive create. Wins → acquired. Non-EEXIST error → caller exits with `reason: "error"` (treats it as in-flight — defensive default). Phase 3 (on EEXIST): `statSync(lockPath)` to check mtime. If `Date.now() - mtimeMs <= ttlMs` → genuinely in-flight; return `acquired: false, reason: "in-flight"`. Otherwise stale (or vanished between the EEXIST and stat — race): `unlinkSync` it, retry the exclusive create. Wins → acquired with `recoveredStale: true`. Loses → `acquired: false, reason: "race"` (extremely rare; an extra "coalesced" log entry isn't a bug). **(4) `releaseInflightLock(lockPath)`** — `unlinkSync` with try/catch; idempotent because both the cleanup-on-exit handler and any normal exit path can call it. Best-effort by design — a unlink failure is harmless because the next acquisition will see the stale mtime and recover. **(5) `main()` integration** — lock acquisition runs after the cache check (so cache hits don't pay the lock cost) but before the codex spawn (so we lock the spawn, not the cheap stuff). TTL is `Math.max(config.timeoutMs, INFLIGHT_TTL_MIN_MS) + 60_000` to handle frontmatter-tuned timeouts (ADR-079). On lock failure: append a `verdict:"skipped"` log entry with `reason: "coalesced — another review is in-flight for this file (<reason>)"` and `process.exit(0)`. On lock success: register `process.on("exit", () => releaseInflightLock(acquiredLockPath))` so the lock is released on every normal exit path — including the existing `process.exit(0)` calls after spawn_failed, timeout, parse_failed, error, and concerns verdicts. SIGKILL bypasses this handler — that's what the TTL stale-recovery is for. **Tests** (`packages/claude-plugin/src/__tests__/codex-pair-watch.test.ts`, 3 new): (i) structural pin asserting the constants, helpers, exclusive-create syscall shape, main() integration call, and cleanup-on-exit registration — locks in the entire wire-up against future refactors. (ii) runtime: pre-create a fresh inflight lock at the expected hash, fire the hook, assert it logs `verdict:"skipped"` with `coalesced` + `in-flight` in the reason AND that the lock file is NOT deleted (the coalesced hook must not release another hook's lock — critical safety property). (iii) runtime: pre-create an inflight lock and `utimes` its mtime to 2 hours ago, fire the hook with PATH-isolated codex (so the hook fails fast after lock acquisition), assert the log has NO `coalesced` entry — proving the stale-recovery path took over. Plugin test count 219 → 222. **Out of scope explicitly**: (a) "re-review after the in-flight one finishes" — the simpler coalesce-without-followup is sufficient: the in-flight reviewer's `buildAdaptiveContext` reads file content at spawn time, so cumulative edits up to that point are reviewed; users who want the final state reviewed can always trigger another edit (or rely on the next non-coalesced edit to pick up the latest content). Followup-review adds complexity (recursive spawning, max-depth bookkeeping, content-hash tracking) for marginal coverage gain. (b) cross-machine locks (e.g. NFS) — the lock is filesystem-local; two developers on different machines naturally have different `.codex-pair-state/` directories and don't share locks. (c) lock-holder PID liveness check via `process.kill(pid, 0)` — PID recycling makes this unsafe; the mtime TTL is the correct staleness signal. (d) priority/preemption — the goal command is coalesce, not "always run the latest". (e) confidence-weighted coalescing ("if the in-flight review is on intermediate state and the new edit is the final state, prefer the new one") — would need to peek into the in-flight reviewer's prompt, which we can't without sharing state.
- **Consequences:** The "rename across 3 files in one turn" false-positive class is closed. Rapid successive edits to the same file produce at most one codex review per file per ~30 seconds, with the in-flight review seeing the cumulative content of all queued edits when it reads the file. Cost drops proportionally: a 3-edit burst on the same file pays for 1 review instead of 3. UX improves: noisy concerns about intermediate state vanish. **What this costs**: (a) every cache-missing hook invocation now does one `mkdirSync` + one `writeFileSync` (atomic exclusive create) + one `statSync` on the unhappy path (lock contention) — sub-millisecond on local filesystems. (b) `process.on("exit", ...)` adds a single listener per hook invocation; the listener is best-effort and never throws, so it can't break the exit. (c) A theoretical race window exists: hook A acquires lock at T=0, the OS bookkeeping for the new file lands at T=1ms, hook B fires at T=0.5ms and sees no lock; both reviews run. Empirically this race window is microseconds wide and acceptable. (d) The user-visible signal that coalescing happened is a `[codex-pair]` skip systemMessage being absent for some edits — by design (silent gating); the `.codex-pair-log.jsonl` shows the `coalesced` reason for forensic visibility. **What this DOESN'T break**: the existing pause sentinel from ADR-085 (it runs before the inflight check, so a paused project sees no inflight locks either); `.codex-pair-ignore` from ADR-081 (also runs first); the cache from ADR-082 (cache hits bypass the inflight check entirely — no spawn means no contention); the verdict closed set (no new verdict; coalescing uses the existing `skipped` verdict with a distinct reason); the log JSONL shape; the silent-on-error contract. **What this enables for downstream work**: (a) story #7 (lib/ extraction) can lift `inflightLockPath`, `tryAcquireInflightLock`, and `releaseInflightLock` into the shared `lib/state.mjs` (or a sibling `lib/coordination.mjs`) module with a clean test surface — the helpers are pure functions with no module-scope state. (b) story #9 (app-server broker) repurposes the same lock primitive to coordinate which session's hook owns a particular review when the broker is the actual executor; the lock semantics carry over unchanged. (c) the future log-viewer can count `verdict:"skipped" reason:"coalesced ..."` entries to surface "X% of your reviews were coalesced" as a cost-savings metric. **Risk explicitly accepted**: the SIGKILL'd-prior-owner case leaves the lock stuck for up to `max(timeoutMs, 600_000) + 60_000` (default ~14 minutes). Until the TTL expires, every edit to that file logs `coalesced` and skips review. The mitigation is the TTL itself — at 10–14 minutes the user gets review back without manual intervention. A user who wants to recover immediately can `rm -rf .codex-pair-state/inflight/` (documented in the SKILL.md update at v0.6.4 release).

## ADR-086: `codex-pair` Hook — Atomic State Writes Audit (Cache `tmp+rename`, Log `reason` Clamping Under `PIPE_BUF`)
- **Date:** 2026-05-19
- **Status:** Accepted (extends ADR-077, ADR-079, ADR-082; v0.6.3 Tier 1 story #5; informed by `openai/codex-plugin-cc` durable-state patterns)
- **Context:** The hook persists three kinds of state to disk: (i) **the cache** — `<markerDir>/.codex-pair-cache/<prefix>/<hash>.json`, one file per content-hash key, written by `setCachedConcerns`, read by `getCachedConcerns` from the same directory (and potentially from sibling Claude sessions if the same project is open in two terminals). (ii) **the log** — `<markerDir>/.codex-pair-log.jsonl`, an append-only JSON-Lines stream that external tools (`tail -f`, post-hoc analysis scripts, the future log viewer in story #9) read while the hook writes. (iii) **the pause sentinel** — `<markerDir>/.codex-pair-state/paused`, a zero-byte marker whose only relevant property is existence; readers `stat`-check it, never read its contents. An audit of the write paths surfaced two non-atomic write surfaces that could produce torn reads: (a) `setCachedConcerns` was a bare `writeFile(cachePath, JSON.stringify(value))` — Node opens with `O_CREAT|O_WRONLY|O_TRUNC`, so a concurrent reader between the truncate and the full write sees an empty or partial file. `getCachedConcerns` already catches the `JSON.parse` failure and falls back to a cache miss (safe-ish), but the *next* read sees the same corrupted file until eviction — the cache entry is dead, every subsequent identical edit pays the full codex spawn until 10-minute TTL expires or the 50-file cap evicts. The bug is real even if its blast radius is small. (b) `appendFile` on a JSON-Lines log file is atomic on POSIX only for writes ≤ `PIPE_BUF` (4096 bytes), per the standard guarantee for `O_APPEND` writes. The hook's log entries are typically a few hundred bytes (timestamp, tool, file path, verdict, counts), but the `reason` field carries codex stderr on error paths and ignore-rule diagnostic strings — those can spike to multi-KB. Two concurrent hook invocations (sibling Claude sessions editing files in the same project) racing on `appendFile` of multi-KB entries could interleave at chunk boundaries, producing a corrupted JSONL line that breaks every reader that parses line-by-line. **Why this matters for downstream work**: story #7 (lib/ extraction) wants to pull `appendLog` and the cache helpers into a shared library; that lib's contract must be "writes are atomic, period" or the consumers will need to reinvent the safety net. Story #9 (app-server broker) will read the log live and feed cache hit-rate metrics — neither use case tolerates torn writes. The pause sentinel from ADR-085 was correct by accident (it's only ever `stat`-ed, never read), and that's actually a useful design lesson: **state writes should be either (1) atomic, or (2) so trivial that readers' only operation is existence-check**. Three design questions: (A) **tmp+rename vs `O_DIRECT`-style sync vs file locks** — tmp+rename is the canonical POSIX/Windows-correct atomic-replace pattern, costs one extra syscall, and degrades gracefully when the filesystem doesn't support cross-directory rename (it won't — the tmp file is in the same directory). `O_SYNC` / fsync is about durability after crash, not atomicity for concurrent reads. File locks (flock/lockf) work but add a coordination surface and need timeouts to avoid stalls — overkill for what's effectively single-writer-per-key. (B) **for the log**, clamping vs locking vs out-of-band serialization (a dedicated writer process) — clamping is the simplest fix that fully solves the problem: bound each entry below PIPE_BUF and `appendFile` is guaranteed atomic by the kernel. Locking introduces stall risk. Out-of-band writer is the broker design from story #9 and is the long-term answer, but not what story #5 asks for. (C) **what max size for `reason`** — `PIPE_BUF` is 4096 bytes; the JSON envelope (timestamp, tool, file path, verdict, level) consumes maybe 400 bytes in the typical case and up to ~600 bytes for very long file paths. Setting `MAX_LOG_REASON_BYTES = 3500` leaves ~600 bytes of headroom for everything else, plus the JSON quoting overhead on the clamped reason itself.
- **Decision:** Two surgical changes to `packages/claude-plugin/scripts/codex-pair-watch.mjs`. **(1) `setCachedConcerns` migrates to tmp+rename** — write to `${cachePath}.tmp.${process.pid}`, then `rename(tmp, final)`. The `${process.pid}` suffix prevents two concurrent hooks (different PIDs) racing on the same cache key from overwriting each other's tmp files mid-stream; one rename wins, the other rename either no-ops (same content) or replaces (different content with newer mtime — also fine because TTL is mtime-driven). The whole operation stays inside the existing `try { ... } catch {}` shell so any failure mode degrades silently (no cache write, next edit pays the spawn cost — same fallback as before). Both `writeFile` and `rename` are awaited so the function doesn't return until the rename has actually landed; otherwise `evictCacheOldest` could readdir before the new entry is visible and over-evict. **(2) `appendLog` clamps `reason`** — new constant `MAX_LOG_REASON_BYTES = 3500` and a tiny `clampReason(reason)` helper. The helper returns the reason unchanged when it's already short or non-string; otherwise it slices to the max and appends `…(${dropped}b truncated)` so the dropped byte count is visible in post-hoc analysis. `appendLog` builds a `safe` entry that swaps the original `reason` for the clamped version when present, then serializes that. The pre-existing fire-and-forget try/catch shell stays in place (logging failures must NEVER break Claude's flow). **No change to** the log filename (`.codex-pair-log.jsonl`), the JSONL line shape (one JSON object per line), the rotation behavior (`rotateLogIfNeeded` already uses tmp+rename — line 837–838 of the script), the cache file layout, the cache key derivation, or the cache TTL/eviction. **Tests** (`packages/claude-plugin/src/__tests__/codex-pair-watch.test.ts`, 3 new): (i) structural pin on `setCachedConcerns` — asserts `.tmp.${process.pid}` suffix, `writeFile(tmpPath, ...)`, `rename(tmpPath, cachePath)`, AND explicitly forbids the bare `writeFile(cachePath, ...)` pattern so a refactor that drops the atomicity fix fails fast; (ii) structural pin on `clampReason` + `MAX_LOG_REASON_BYTES` + the `appendLog` call site routing reason through `clampReason`; (iii) sizing test — derives the constant from the source, simulates clamping a 10 KB reason, builds a realistic log envelope (long file path, all standard fields), and asserts the full JSON envelope length is under `PIPE_BUF = 4096`. Plugin test count 216 → 219. **Out of scope explicitly**: (a) lock-based serialization between concurrent hook invocations — overkill given clamp+atomic gives us the contracts we need; (b) `fsync` for durability after machine crash — these are local-developer caches/logs that get re-derived after a crash, durability isn't a property we need; (c) Windows-specific atomicity verification — Node's `fs.rename` uses `MoveFileEx(MOVEFILE_REPLACE_EXISTING)` on Windows which is atomic; we trust the runtime here; (d) compacting old log entries with multi-KB stderr — rotation already trims the file at MAX_LOG_BYTES, and clamping bounds individual entries; the combination is sufficient; (e) the pause sentinel from ADR-085 was audited and required no change (existence-check only).
- **Consequences:** The cache is now safe under concurrent read/write across sibling Claude sessions — a reader catching the writer mid-flight either sees the previous full file (rename hasn't landed) or the new full file (rename has landed), never a torn intermediate. The torn-cache-poisoning bug (one bad write → 10 minutes of bypassed cache for that key) is closed. The log file is now guaranteed atomic per entry on POSIX (`O_APPEND` write ≤ `PIPE_BUF`) and effectively safe on Windows where Node's append goes through a single `WriteFile` call. **What this costs**: (a) every cache write now does one extra `rename` syscall — sub-millisecond, negligible against the codex spawn cost the cache exists to avoid. (b) cache writes that race for the same key now produce a tiny window where two tmp files coexist; the second `rename` wins and the first's tmp is silently overwritten — no leak because rename of N tmps to the same final path leaves zero stragglers. (c) log entries with reasons over 3500 bytes are visibly truncated with a byte count in the file — forensic readers lose the tail of long stderr blobs, but the alternative was a corrupted JSONL line that wrecks every parser downstream. The `…(${N}b truncated)` marker makes the truncation observable. **What this DOESN'T change**: the public log JSONL shape (still one object per line with the same fields); the cache content shape (still `{high, med, low, durationMs}`); the cache key derivation (still sha256 of model+prompt+fileContent+threshold); the rotation thresholds (still `MAX_LOG_BYTES = 2_000_000` and `MAX_LOG_ENTRIES = 1000`); the hook's silent-on-error contract (every state-write failure path still degrades silently). The pause sentinel from ADR-085 is unaffected. The `.codex-pair-ignore` matcher from ADR-081 is unaffected. **What this enables for downstream work**: (a) story #7 (lib/ extraction) can now lift `setCachedConcerns`, `appendLog`, `clampReason`, and the cache key helpers into a shared module and expose them with a clean "writes are atomic" contract, with no caveats for consumers. (b) story #9 (app-server broker) can plug a live JSONL reader (`tail -f` semantics) onto the log without worrying about torn lines breaking the parser; cache-hit-rate metrics computed from the log JSONL stream will be correct. (c) future cache shapes (e.g. a per-project metadata file) can clone the tmp+rename pattern from `setCachedConcerns` instead of reinventing it.

## ADR-085: `codex-pair` Hook — `/codex-pair-pause` and `/codex-pair-resume` Slash Commands via Sentinel File
- **Date:** 2026-05-19
- **Status:** Accepted (extends ADR-077, ADR-079, ADR-081; v0.6.3 Tier 1 story #5b)
- **Context:** Before this change, codex-pair's on/off control had three blunt knobs: (a) `rm .codex-pair-context.md` to permanently disable (destroys the project's context), (b) `/plugin disable ask-llm` to disable for the whole session (kills every other ask-llm command too — `/codex-review`, `/brainstorm`, the MCP servers), (c) `CODEX_PAIR_DISABLED=1 <cmd>` per-process kill switch (only for that single command, doesn't persist across hook invocations from Claude). None of those map onto the most common reason to *temporarily* silence review: "I'm running a noisy refactor for the next 30 minutes, mute it." The user can't realistically env-var every Edit Claude is about to issue from inside an active session, the session-kill is too coarse, and removing the marker file loses the project context (model, severity threshold, security rules) that took deliberate effort to write. The right primitive is a **pause sentinel** — a small artifact whose mere existence flips the hook from "review" to "skip", that lives next to the marker but is orthogonal to it. The marker stays put (project is *configured*); the sentinel toggles whether review actually runs (project is *paused*). Two slash commands manage the sentinel from inside Claude Code without forcing the user to remember filesystem paths. **Design questions**: (A) **storage shape** — single sentinel file (`.codex-pair-state/paused`), a JSON config, or a directory whose presence is the switch?
